**Module 15 -- Lesson 1**

**Role of Modern Business Analyst**

**1. What is a Business Analyst?**

A Business Analyst is a professional who connects:

Business Needs

↓

Analysis

↓

Requirements

↓

Technology Solution

↓

Business Value

Simple definition:

A Business Analyst identifies business problems, understands stakeholder
needs, defines requirements, and helps teams deliver the right solution.

**2. Old BA vs Modern BA**

**Traditional BA**

Mostly:

-   Gather requirements

-   Write documents

-   Coordinate meetings

**Modern BA**

Works as:

-   Problem solver

-   Product thinker

-   Data analyst

-   Process analyst

-   Customer advocate

Modern BA asks:

Not:

\"What feature do you want?\"

But:

\"Why do you need this? What problem are we solving?\"

**3. BA as a Bridge**

A BA connects multiple worlds:

Business Team

↕

Business Analyst

↕

Development Team

↕

Users

Example:

Business says:

\"We need AI automation.\"

Developer asks:

\"What exactly should AI do?\"

BA converts vague idea into:

-   Requirements

-   Rules

-   Workflow

-   Acceptance criteria

**4. BA Role in Product Development**

**Discovery Phase**

BA helps identify:

-   User problems

-   Business goals

-   Current process

**Analysis Phase**

BA creates:

-   Process maps

-   Requirements

-   Gap analysis

**Development Phase**

BA supports:

-   Developer clarification

-   Requirement changes

**Testing Phase**

BA helps:

-   UAT

-   Validation

**Release Phase**

BA supports:

-   Documentation

-   Training

-   Adoption

**5. BA Responsibilities**

**Requirement Management**

Understand:

\"What should the system do?\"

**Process Analysis**

Understand:

\"How does work happen today?\"

**Stakeholder Management**

Understand:

\"Who needs what?\"

**Solution Evaluation**

Understand:

\"Is this solution valuable?\"

**Communication**

Translate:

Business language ↔ Technical language

**6. BA Skills Framework**

A modern BA needs:

**Analytical Skills**

-   Problem solving

-   RCA

-   Impact analysis

**Documentation Skills**

-   BRD

-   FRD

-   User stories

**Modeling Skills**

-   BPMN

-   UML

-   Workflow design

**Product Skills**

-   User research

-   Metrics

-   Prioritization

**Technical Understanding**

-   APIs

-   Databases

-   AI concepts

**7. Healthcare BA Example**

Problem:

Pharma company wants AI-based safety automation.

Business statement:

\"We need AI to improve pharmacovigilance.\"

BA questions:

**Current Process**

How are cases processed today?

**Users**

Who reviews cases?

**Pain Points**

Where is time wasted?

**Requirements**

What should AI do?

**Success Metrics**

How will we know it worked?

**8. BA Deliverables**

A BA produces artifacts like:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stage**                           **Document**
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Discovery                           Business Case

  Analysis                            Gap Analysis

  Requirements                        BRD

  Solution                            FRD

  Design                              Workflow

  Development                         User Stories

  Testing                             UAT Cases
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**9. BA Interview Question**

**Question:**

\"What is the role of a Business Analyst?\"

Strong answer:

\"A Business Analyst acts as a bridge between business stakeholders and
delivery teams. The BA identifies business problems, analyzes processes,
gathers and documents requirements, and ensures the delivered solution
creates measurable business value.\"

Today you learned:

✅ BA role across product lifecycle\
✅ BA responsibilities\
✅ BA skills framework\
✅ Healthcare BA example\
✅ BA deliverables

**Lesson 2: Business Problem Identification & Problem Framing**

**A great BA does not start with requirements. A great BA starts by
understanding the real problem.**

Many failed projects happen because teams build the wrong solution.

Example:

Business says:

\"We need an AI chatbot.\"

A weak BA asks:

\"What chatbot features do you need?\"

A strong BA asks:

\"What business problem are we trying to solve?\"

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   Difference between problem and solution

-   Problem identification techniques

-   Problem statement creation

-   Problem vs symptom analysis

-   5 Whys technique

-   Problem Tree Analysis

-   Business impact analysis

-   Converting problems into opportunities

-   Healthcare/pharma examples

**1. Problem vs Solution**

**Solution Thinking**

A stakeholder says:

\"We need a mobile app.\"

The team immediately starts designing.

Problem:

Nobody knows why the app is needed.

**Problem Thinking**

A BA asks:

Why do users need a mobile app?

Possible answer:

\"Patients cannot easily track medication schedules.\"

Now the real problem is identified.

**Example**

**Statement 1:**

\"We need an AI system.\"

This is a solution.

**Statement 2:**

\"Safety reviewers spend 40% of their time manually extracting
information from adverse event reports.\"

This is a problem.

A BA works on Statement 2.

**2. Types of Business Problems**

Business problems usually fall into categories.

**1. Efficiency Problems**

Goal:

Reduce time and effort.

Example:

Manual data entry takes 5 hours daily.

**2. Quality Problems**

Goal:

Improve accuracy.

Example:

Incorrect medical coding.

**3. Cost Problems**

Goal:

Reduce expenses.

Example:

High operational cost.

**4. Customer Problems**

Goal:

Improve user experience.

Example:

Patients cannot access reports easily.

**5. Compliance Problems**

Goal:

Meet regulations.

Example:

Missing audit documentation.

**6. Growth Problems**

Goal:

Increase revenue/adoption.

Example:

Low product usage.

**3. Problem Identification Process**

A BA follows:

Observe Current Situation

↓

Identify Pain Points

↓

Analyze Root Cause

↓

Measure Impact

↓

Define Problem Statement

↓

Find Solution Options

**Step 1: Understand Current State**

Questions:

-   How does work happen today?

-   Who performs it?

-   What tools are used?

-   Where are delays?

Example:

Pharmacovigilance workflow:

Current:

Reviewer manually reads 50-page reports.

**Step 2: Identify Pain Points**

Pain points:

-   Too much manual work

-   Slow processing

-   Repetitive activities

-   Error risk

**Step 3: Analyze Root Cause**

Don\'t stop at symptoms.

Example:

Problem:

\"Case processing is slow.\"

Possible causes:

-   Poor document quality

-   Manual extraction

-   Multiple approvals

-   Lack of automation

**Step 4: Measure Impact**

A problem becomes important when impact is clear.

Example:

Current:

1000 cases/month

Average review time:

30 minutes

Total effort:

500 hours/month

**Step 5: Write Problem Statement**

A good problem statement includes:

**Who?**

Affected user.

**What?**

Problem.

**Impact?**

Business consequence.

**Evidence?**

Data.

**Problem Statement Template**

\"\[User group\] faces \[specific problem\], resulting in \[business
impact\]. Currently, \[evidence/data\].\"

**Example: Healthcare AI**

Weak:

\"Pharmacovigilance needs AI.\"

Strong:

\"Pharmacovigilance reviewers spend significant time manually extracting
information from adverse event reports, causing delayed case processing
and increased operational workload. Current processing requires
approximately 30 minutes per case.\"

**4. Problem Framing Framework**

Use:

**5W1H Framework**

**What?**

What is happening?

Example:

Case review takes too long.

**Who?**

Who experiences the problem?

Example:

Safety reviewers.

**Where?**

Where does it happen?

Example:

Drug safety operations.

**When?**

When does it happen?

Example:

During case intake.

**Why?**

Why does it matter?

Example:

Delays regulatory reporting.

**How?**

How is it currently handled?

Example:

Manual review.

**5. Problem vs Symptom**

Very important BA skill.

**Symptom**

What we observe.

**Root Problem**

Why it happens.

Example:

Symptom:

\"Customers abandon checkout.\"

Possible root problems:

-   Slow payment process

-   Too many fields

-   Lack of trust

Healthcare Example:

Symptom:

\"Safety team misses deadlines.\"

Possible root problems:

-   Manual processes

-   Poor prioritization

-   Lack of automation

**6. The 5 Whys Technique**

Created by:

Toyota Production System

Purpose:

Find root cause.

**Example:**

Problem:

Safety reports are delayed.

**Why 1:**

Why are reports delayed?

Because reviewers take too long to process cases.

**Why 2:**

Why does processing take long?

Because information extraction is manual.

**Why 3:**

Why is extraction manual?

Because documents come in different formats.

**Why 4:**

Why are formats inconsistent?

Because sources are not standardized.

**Why 5:**

Why are sources not standardized?

Because there is no centralized intake process.

**Root Cause:**

Lack of standardized intake workflow.

**7. Problem Tree Analysis**

A problem tree separates:

**Causes**

↓

**Core Problem**

↓

**Effects**

Example:

Effects

Delayed Regulatory Submission

↑

CORE PROBLEM

Slow Pharmacovigilance Processing

↑

Causes

Manual Data Extraction

Multiple Data Sources

Poor Workflow

**8. Business Impact Analysis**

Every problem should be connected to impact.

**Operational Impact**

Example:

More manual hours.

**Financial Impact**

Example:

Higher processing cost.

**Customer Impact**

Example:

Poor user experience.

**Compliance Impact**

Example:

Regulatory risk.

**Example:**

Problem:

Manual PV review.

Impact:

Operational:

500 extra hours/month.

Financial:

Higher staffing cost.

Compliance:

Risk of delayed submissions.

**9. Converting Problems Into Opportunities**

A BA transforms:

Problem → Opportunity

Example:

Problem:

\"Reviewers spend too much time reading reports.\"

Opportunity:

\"Create AI-assisted document analysis capability.\"

Problem:

\"Patients forget medication schedules.\"

Opportunity:

\"Create digital medication adherence assistant.\"

**10. Problem Prioritization**

Not every problem should be solved.

Use:

**Impact vs Effort Matrix**

HIGH IMPACT

\|

\|

Quick Wins \| Strategic Projects

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--\|\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

\|

Low Priority \| Avoid

\|

LOW IMPACT

LOW EFFORT → HIGH EFFORT

**Example:**

AI Summary Feature:

Impact:

High

Effort:

Medium

Priority:

High

**11. BA Interview Questions**

**Q1: How do you identify business problems?**

Answer:

\"I start by understanding the current process, interviewing
stakeholders, analyzing pain points, collecting data, identifying root
causes, and defining the problem with measurable business impact before
discussing solutions.\"

**Q2: Difference between problem and requirement?**

Answer:

Problem:

Why change is needed.

Requirement:

What the solution must provide.

Example:

Problem:

Case review takes too long.

Requirement:

System should automatically summarize reports.

**12. Practical Exercise**

**Scenario:**

A pharmaceutical company says:

\"We need AI automation for pharmacovigilance.\"

As BA, analyze:

**Step 1: Ask:**

Who has the problem?

**Step 2:**

What process is inefficient?

**Step 3:**

What is the root cause?

**Step 4:**

What is the business impact?

**Step 5:**

Write problem statement.

**Assignment**

Create a **Problem Analysis Document** for AI Pharmacovigilance
Platform.

Include:

1.  Current situation

2.  Stakeholders affected

3.  Pain points

4.  Problem statement

5.  5 Whys analysis

6.  Root cause

7.  Business impact

8.  Opportunity statement

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ BA starts with problems, not solutions\
✅ How to identify business problems\
✅ Problem framing techniques\
✅ Problem vs symptom\
✅ 5 Whys analysis\
✅ Problem tree analysis\
✅ Impact analysis basics\
✅ Converting problems into opportunities

**Lesson 3: Stakeholder Analysis & Management Masterclass**

**A BA does not manage only requirements. A BA manages people,
expectations, conflicts, and alignment.**

Many projects fail not because of technology problems, but because:

-   The wrong people were involved

-   Expectations were unclear

-   Stakeholders were not aligned

-   Important feedback came too late

A professional BA learns:

\"Who matters, what they need, and how to keep them engaged.\"

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What stakeholders are

-   Types of stakeholders

-   Stakeholder identification techniques

-   Stakeholder analysis

-   Power-Interest Matrix

-   Influence mapping

-   RACI Matrix

-   Communication planning

-   Managing difficult stakeholders

-   Healthcare AI stakeholder example

**1. What is a Stakeholder?**

A stakeholder is:

Any person, group, or organization that can affect or is affected by a
project.

Simple:

Anyone who can:

-   Influence the project

-   Use the product

-   Approve decisions

-   Provide information

-   Be impacted by changes

**Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

Stakeholders include:

-   Pharmacovigilance reviewers

-   Medical reviewers

-   Safety managers

-   Regulatory team

-   IT team

-   Data scientists

-   Compliance team

-   Executives

**2. Why Stakeholder Management Matters**

Imagine building an AI safety assistant.

Development team creates:

\"Automatic case approval.\"

But regulatory team says:

\"AI cannot approve safety cases without human review.\"

Problem:

Stakeholder was not involved early.

A BA prevents this by understanding:

-   Needs

-   Concerns

-   Authority

-   Expectations

**3. Types of Stakeholders**

**Internal Stakeholders**

Inside the organization.

Examples:

-   Product team

-   Engineering

-   Quality team

-   Management

**External Stakeholders**

Outside organization.

Examples:

-   Customers

-   Vendors

-   Regulators

-   Partners

**4. Stakeholder Categories**

**1. Business Stakeholders**

Focus:

Business value.

Examples:

-   Product Manager

-   Business Owner

-   Executives

Questions:

\"Will this achieve business goals?\"

**2. User Stakeholders**

Focus:

Using the product.

Examples:

-   Pharmacovigilance reviewer

-   Doctor

-   Patient

Questions:

\"Will this solve my problem?\"

**3. Technical Stakeholders**

Focus:

Building solution.

Examples:

-   Developers

-   Architects

-   Data engineers

Questions:

\"Can we build it?\"

**4. Governance Stakeholders**

Focus:

Risk and compliance.

Examples:

-   Legal

-   Regulatory

-   Security

Questions:

\"Is it safe and compliant?\"

**5. Stakeholder Identification Process**

A BA follows:

Project Objective

↓

Identify People Affected

↓

Identify Decision Makers

↓

Identify Experts

↓

Create Stakeholder Register

**6. Stakeholder Register**

A stakeholder register documents:

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stakeholder**           **Role**          **Interest**   **Influence**
  ------------------------- ----------------- -------------- ---------------
  PV Reviewer               User              High           Medium

  Safety Manager            Sponsor           High           High

  IT Manager                Technical         Medium         High

  Regulatory Team           Compliance        High           High
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

**7. Stakeholder Analysis**

The BA analyzes:

**Interest**

How much do they care?

**Influence**

How much power do they have?

**Impact**

How will they be affected?

**8. Power-Interest Matrix**

One of the most important BA tools.

Matrix:

HIGH POWER

\|

\|

Manage \| Manage Closely

Closely \|

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--\|\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

\|

Monitor \| Keep Informed

\|

\|

LOW POWER

LOW INTEREST \-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-- HIGH INTEREST

**Category 1: Manage Closely**

High Power + High Interest

Need:

Frequent communication.

Examples:

-   Product Sponsor

-   Safety Director

**Category 2: Keep Satisfied**

High Power + Low Interest

Need:

Regular updates.

Example:

Senior executives.

**Category 3: Keep Informed**

Low Power + High Interest

Need:

Information.

Example:

End users.

**Category 4: Monitor**

Low Power + Low Interest

Need:

Minimal communication.

**9. Healthcare AI Example**

**AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Safety Head**

Power:

High

Interest:

High

Action:

Weekly updates.

**Reviewer**

Power:

Medium

Interest:

High

Action:

Feedback sessions.

**CEO**

Power:

High

Interest:

Low

Action:

Monthly business updates.

**AI Engineer**

Power:

Medium

Interest:

High

Action:

Technical discussions.

**10. RACI Matrix**

RACI defines responsibilities.

R:

Responsible

Person doing the work.

A:

Accountable

Person who owns the decision.

C:

Consulted

Provides expertise.

I:

Informed

Needs updates.

**Example:**

Feature:

AI Summary Generation

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Activity**     **BA**   **Product Manager**   **Developer**   **PV Reviewer**
  ---------------- -------- --------------------- --------------- ---------------
  Requirement      R        A                     C               C

  Development      C        A                     R               C

  Testing          C        A                     C               R

  Approval         I        A                     I               C
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------

**11. Communication Plan**

A BA creates a communication strategy.

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stakeholder**         **Information**            **Frequency**
  ----------------------- -------------------------- --------------------
  Sponsor                 Progress                   Monthly

  Users                   Feedback                   Weekly

  Developers              Requirements               Daily

  Compliance              Risk updates               Biweekly
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**12. Stakeholder Interview Techniques**

Good BA questions:

**For Users:**

\"What challenges do you face today?\"

**For Managers:**

\"What business outcome do you expect?\"

**For Developers:**

\"What technical limitations exist?\"

**For Compliance:**

\"What regulations must we satisfy?\"

**13. Managing Difficult Stakeholders**

Common situations:

**Situation 1:**

Stakeholder changes requirements frequently.

Solution:

-   Understand reason

-   Document change

-   Perform impact analysis

-   Prioritize

**Situation 2:**

Stakeholder does not participate.

Solution:

-   Explain impact

-   Schedule focused sessions

-   Provide clear decisions needed

**Situation 3:**

Two stakeholders disagree.

Solution:

-   Understand objectives

-   Use data

-   Facilitate decision-making

**Example:**

Medical team wants:

Maximum safety checks.

Business team wants:

Faster processing.

BA solution:

Create balanced workflow:

AI assists speed + human approval maintains safety.

**14. Stakeholder Management in Agile**

In Agile:

Stakeholders are continuously involved.

They participate in:

-   Sprint reviews

-   Backlog refinement

-   User testing

-   Feedback sessions

BA/Product Owner role:

Convert feedback into actionable requirements.

**15. Stakeholder Management Interview Questions**

**Q1:**

How do you identify stakeholders?

Answer:

\"I identify stakeholders by analyzing the business objective, impacted
users, decision makers, technical teams, and governance groups. I
document them in a stakeholder register and analyze their influence and
interest.\"

**Q2:**

How do you handle conflicting stakeholder requirements?

Answer:

\"I understand each stakeholder\'s objective, analyze business impact,
evaluate trade-offs, and facilitate a decision based on value,
feasibility, and project goals.\"

**Q3:**

What is RACI?

Answer:

\"RACI defines responsibility ownership by identifying who is
Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, and Informed for project
activities.\"

**Lesson 4: Business Analysis Planning & BA Approach**

**A professional BA does not start collecting requirements immediately.
First, they plan how the analysis will be performed.**

Many beginner BAs make this mistake:

❌ \"Let\'s schedule interviews and collect requirements.\"

A senior BA thinks:

✅ \"What is the business objective? Who should be involved? What
analysis approach should we use? What outputs are expected?\"

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What BA planning means

-   BA approach definition

-   BA activities planning

-   Scope definition

-   Requirement planning

-   Stakeholder engagement planning

-   Analysis technique selection

-   BA documentation plan

-   Agile BA planning

**1. What is Business Analysis Planning?**

Business Analysis Planning defines:

How the BA work will be performed to understand the business problem and
deliver the right solution.

It answers:

-   What are we analyzing?

-   Why are we analyzing it?

-   Who will participate?

-   What techniques will we use?

-   What documents will be created?

-   How will decisions be made?

**2. Why BA Planning Is Important**

Without planning:

-   Wrong stakeholders may be missed

-   Requirements may be incomplete

-   Analysis may take longer

-   Teams may build the wrong solution

Example:

A pharma company says:

\"We want AI automation.\"

Without planning:

Team starts building chatbot.

With BA planning:

BA discovers:

Actual need:

\"Reduce manual adverse event data extraction.\"

**3. BA Planning Process**

A typical BA planning cycle:

Business Objective

↓

Define Scope

↓

Identify Stakeholders

↓

Select Analysis Approach

↓

Plan Activities

↓

Define Deliverables

↓

Execute Analysis

**4. Define Business Objective**

Before analysis, understand:

**Why does this project exist?**

Example:

Business Goal:

Reduce pharmacovigilance processing time by 40%.

BA translates this into analysis objectives:

-   Understand current workflow

-   Identify bottlenecks

-   Define automation opportunities

**5. Define Scope**

Scope defines:

**What is included?**

and

**What is not included?**

Example:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**In Scope:**

✅ Case summarization\
✅ Data extraction\
✅ Coding assistance

**Out of Scope:**

❌ Final medical decision making\
❌ Autonomous regulatory submission

**Scope Creep**

A common BA challenge.

Example:

Initial request:

\"AI summary generator.\"

Later requests:

-   AI chatbot

-   Dashboard

-   Prediction model

-   Mobile app

-   Analytics

Problem:

Project expands without control.

BA manages this through:

-   Change requests

-   Impact analysis

-   Prioritization

**6. BA Approach Selection**

Different projects require different approaches.

**Approach 1: Predictive / Waterfall BA**

Used when:

Requirements are stable.

Example:

Regulatory systems.

Process:

Requirements

↓

Design

↓

Development

↓

Testing

↓

Release

**Approach 2: Agile BA**

Used when:

Requirements evolve.

Example:

AI products.

Process:

Discovery

↓

Backlog

↓

Sprint

↓

Feedback

↓

Improvement

**Approach 3: Hybrid BA**

Combination.

Example:

Healthcare enterprise AI.

Fixed:

-   Compliance requirements

Flexible:

-   AI features

**7. BA Activities Planning**

A BA plans activities like:

**Stakeholder Interviews**

Purpose:

Understand needs.

**Workshops**

Purpose:

Collaborative requirement discovery.

**Process Analysis**

Purpose:

Understand current workflow.

**Document Analysis**

Purpose:

Review existing information.

**Observation**

Purpose:

Understand real user behavior.

**Prototyping**

Purpose:

Validate ideas.

**8. Requirement Planning**

A BA decides:

**Requirement Sources**

Where will information come from?

Examples:

-   Users

-   Existing documents

-   Systems

-   Regulations

**Requirement Types**

**Business Requirements**

Why?

Example:

Reduce processing time.

**Functional Requirements**

What should system do?

Example:

Generate case summary.

**Non-Functional Requirements**

How well?

Example:

Response time \< 5 seconds.

**9. Deliverables Planning**

A BA defines expected outputs.

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Activity**                          **Deliverable**
  ------------------------------------- ---------------------------------
  Stakeholder analysis                  Stakeholder register

  Process analysis                      BPMN workflow

  Requirement gathering                 BRD

  Functional analysis                   FRD

  User requirements                     User stories

  Testing                               UAT cases
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**10. BA Tools and Techniques Selection**

A BA selects tools based on need.

**Problem Understanding**

Tools:

-   5 Whys

-   Problem Tree

-   SWOT

**Process Understanding**

Tools:

-   BPMN

-   Flowcharts

-   Value Stream Mapping

**Requirement Discovery**

Tools:

-   Interviews

-   Workshops

-   Surveys

**Solution Validation**

Tools:

-   Wireframes

-   Prototypes

-   User testing

**11. Stakeholder Engagement Plan**

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Project:

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stakeholder**               **Method**         **Frequency**
  ----------------------------- ------------------ -----------------------
  PV Reviewers                  Workshop           Weekly

  Product Owner                 Meeting            Twice weekly

  Regulatory Team               Review             Biweekly

  Developers                    Discussion         Daily
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

**12. BA Planning Document Structure**

A BA Approach Document usually contains:

**1. Project Overview**

Purpose and objectives.

**2. Scope**

In scope/out of scope.

**3. Stakeholders**

Roles and responsibilities.

**4. Analysis Approach**

Methods and techniques.

**5. Activities**

Timeline and tasks.

**6. Deliverables**

Documents to produce.

**7. Communication Plan**

Stakeholder interaction.

**8. Risks**

Potential challenges.

**13. Agile BA Planning**

In Agile environments, BA planning is continuous.

BA works with:

-   Product Owner

-   Scrum Master

-   Development Team

Activities:

**Before Sprint**

-   Refine backlog

-   Clarify requirements

**During Sprint**

-   Answer questions

-   Clarify acceptance criteria

**After Sprint**

-   Gather feedback

-   Improve backlog

**14. Healthcare AI Example: BA Approach Plan**

**Project:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

**Objective:**

Reduce case review effort.

**Analysis Approach:**

Agile + Human-centered design.

**Techniques:**

-   Stakeholder interviews

-   Workflow mapping

-   Prototyping

-   User stories

-   BPMN workflow

-   AI requirements

-   UAT scenarios

**15. BA Interview Questions**

**Q1:**

How do you plan BA activities?

Answer:

\"I start by understanding business objectives, defining scope,
identifying stakeholders, selecting appropriate analysis techniques,
planning activities, and defining expected deliverables.\"

**Q2:**

What factors decide your BA approach?

Answer:

\"I consider project complexity, requirement stability, stakeholder
availability, regulatory needs, and delivery methodology such as Agile,
Waterfall, or Hybrid.\"

**Q3:**

How do you handle changing requirements?

Answer:

\"I evaluate the impact of changes, discuss priority with stakeholders,

**Lesson 5: Gap Analysis Masterclass**

**Gap Analysis is one of the most important tools in a Business
Analyst's toolkit because it answers one fundamental question:**

**\"Where are we today, where do we want to be, and what needs to change
to reach there?\"**

A BA does not directly jump into solution design.

First, they understand the **gap between the current state and desired
future state.**

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What Gap Analysis is

-   Why BAs use Gap Analysis

-   AS-IS vs TO-BE analysis

-   Types of gaps

-   Gap identification techniques

-   Capability Gap Analysis

-   Process Gap Analysis

-   Technology Gap Analysis

-   Organizational Gap Analysis

-   Gap Analysis document creation

-   Healthcare AI example

**1. What is Gap Analysis?**

Definition:

Gap Analysis is a technique used to compare the current state of a
business process, system, or capability with the desired future state to
identify missing elements that must be improved.

Simple formula:

Current State (AS-IS)

\+

Future State (TO-BE)

=

Gap

↓

Improvement Actions

**2. Why Gap Analysis Is Important**

Without Gap Analysis:

Teams may build unnecessary features.

Example:

Business says:

\"We need AI automation.\"

BA investigates.

Current problem:

Only 20% of reports are standardized.

AI cannot work effectively.

Actual need:

Improve data quality first.

**3. AS-IS vs TO-BE Analysis**

This is the foundation of Gap Analysis.

**AS-IS State**

Meaning:

How things work today.

Questions:

-   What is the current process?

-   Who performs activities?

-   What tools are used?

-   What problems exist?

Example:

**Pharmacovigilance Current Process**

Receive Report

↓

Manual Reading

↓

Manual Data Extraction

↓

Manual Coding

↓

Manual Narrative Writing

↓

Approval

Problems:

-   Slow

-   Manual effort

-   Error risk

**TO-BE State**

Meaning:

How the future process should work.

Questions:

-   What improvement is expected?

-   What capabilities are needed?

-   What technology supports it?

Example:

**Future AI-Assisted Process**

Receive Report

↓

AI Extracts Information

↓

AI Generates Summary

↓

Reviewer Validates

↓

Approval

**4. The Gap**

The difference:

AS-IS

Manual extraction

↓

GAP

Need AI extraction capability

↓

TO-BE

Automated extraction

**5. Types of Gap Analysis**

A BA analyzes different gaps.

**1. Process Gap**

Difference between current workflow and desired workflow.

Example:

Current:

Manual case review.

Future:

AI-assisted review.

Gap:

Automation capability missing.

**2. Technology Gap**

Difference between current technology and required technology.

Example:

Current:

Basic database.

Required:

AI platform + API integration.

Gap:

Technology infrastructure missing.

**3. Data Gap**

Difference between available data and required data.

Example:

AI requires:

-   Historical cases

-   Medical terminology

-   Regulatory documents

Current:

Incomplete datasets.

Gap:

Data preparation needed.

**4. Skill Gap**

Difference between current skills and required skills.

Example:

Current:

Safety team knows PV processes.

Needed:

AI workflow knowledge.

Gap:

AI training required.

**5. Capability Gap**

Difference between current business ability and future capability.

Example:

Current capability:

Process cases manually.

Future capability:

Predict safety signals using AI.

Gap:

AI analytics capability.

**6. Organizational Gap**

Difference in:

-   Roles

-   Processes

-   Culture

Example:

Employees do not trust AI.

Gap:

Change management required.

**6. Gap Analysis Framework**

A common BA template:

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Area**     **Current       **Future State**  **Gap**        **Action**
               State**                                          
  ------------ --------------- ----------------- -------------- --------------
  Process      Manual review   AI-assisted       Automation     Build AI
                               review            missing        workflow

  Data         Scattered       Central knowledge Data           Create data
               documents       base              integration    pipeline
                                                 gap            

  Technology   Legacy system   AI platform       Missing AI     Add AI
                                                 layer          services

  Skills       Manual          AI collaboration  Training gap   Conduct
               expertise                                        training
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------

**7. Gap Analysis Process**

A BA follows:

Define Objective

↓

Analyze Current State

↓

Define Future State

↓

Identify Gaps

↓

Prioritize Gaps

↓

Create Action Plan

**Step 1: Define Objective**

Example:

Improve pharmacovigilance processing efficiency.

**Step 2: Analyze Current State**

Methods:

-   Interviews

-   Observation

-   Document review

-   Process mapping

**Step 3: Define Future State**

Example:

AI-powered safety workflow.

**Step 4: Identify Gaps**

Ask:

\"What prevents us from reaching the future state?\"

**Step 5: Prioritize Gaps**

Not every gap has equal importance.

Use:

**Impact vs Effort Matrix**

HIGH IMPACT

\|

Quick Wins \| Strategic Improvements

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--\|\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

\|

Low Priority \| Avoid

\|

LOW IMPACT

**8. Gap Analysis Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Business Goal:**

Reduce adverse event case processing time by 50%.

**Current State (AS-IS)**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Area**                   **Current Situation**
  -------------------------- --------------------------------------------
  Intake                     Emails and PDFs

  Extraction                 Manual

  Coding                     Manual

  Narrative                  Human-written

  Reporting                  Manual
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Future State (TO-BE)**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Area**          **Future Capability**
  ----------------- -----------------------------------------------------
  Intake            Automated document processing

  Extraction        AI extraction

  Coding            AI suggestions

  Narrative         AI-generated draft

  Reporting         Workflow automation
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Identified Gaps**

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Gap**                                       **Impact**   **Priority**
  --------------------------------------------- ------------ --------------
  No AI extraction                              High         Critical

  Poor document standardization                 High         Critical

  No AI governance                              High         High

  Limited user training                         Medium       Medium
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------

**9. Gap Analysis Document Structure**

A professional BA document:

**1. Introduction**

Purpose of analysis.

Example:

\"Analyze current pharmacovigilance workflow and identify improvements
required for AI implementation.\"

**2. Current State Analysis**

Describe:

-   Processes

-   Systems

-   People

-   Problems

**3. Future State Vision**

Describe:

-   Desired capabilities

-   Target workflow

**4. Gap Identification**

Document differences.

**5. Gap Prioritization**

Rank:

-   Critical

-   High

-   Medium

-   Low

**6. Recommendations**

Define improvement actions.

**10. Gap Analysis vs Root Cause Analysis**

Important interview distinction.

**Gap Analysis**

Question:

\"What is missing?\"

Example:

AI capability missing.

**Root Cause Analysis**

Question:

\"Why does the problem exist?\"

Example:

Manual process exists because legacy systems were never upgraded.

Both are often used together.

**11. Gap Analysis in Agile**

In Agile, Gap Analysis helps create:

-   Epics

-   Features

-   User stories

Example:

Gap:

Manual extraction exists.

↓

Epic:

AI Document Processing.

↓

User Story:

\"As a reviewer, I want AI to extract case information so that I reduce
manual work.\"

**12. Interview Questions**

**Q1: What is Gap Analysis?**

Answer:

\"Gap Analysis compares the current state with the desired future state
to identify missing capabilities, processes, technology, or resources
required to achieve business objectives.\"

**Q2: How do you perform Gap Analysis?**

Answer:

\"I analyze the current state, define the target state, identify
differences, prioritize gaps based on impact, and create
recommendations.\"

**Q3: Give an example.**

Answer:

\"In a pharmacovigilance AI project, the current process involved manual
case review. The future state required AI-assisted review. The gaps
included missing AI capabilities, data preparation needs, and user
training requirements.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a **Gap Analysis Document** for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot**

Include:

**Current State**

-   Process

-   Technology

-   Data

-   People

**Future State**

-   AI capabilities

-   Workflow improvements

**Gap Table**

Columns:

1.  Area

2.  Current State

3.  Future State

4.  Gap

5.  Priority

6.  Recommendation

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ What Gap Analysis is\
✅ AS-IS vs TO-BE analysis\
✅ Types of gaps\
✅ Gap identification process\
✅ Gap prioritization\
✅ Gap Analysis documentation\
✅ Healthcare AI example\
✅ Difference between Gap Analysis and RCA

**Lesson 6: Root Cause Analysis (RCA) Masterclass**

**A professional BA does not solve symptoms. A professional BA finds the
real reason behind the problem.**

Organizations often waste money because they treat the visible issue
instead of fixing the actual cause.

Example:

Problem:

\"Pharmacovigilance cases are delayed.\"

A weak solution:

\"Add more reviewers.\"

A BA asks:

\"Why are cases delayed?\"

Possible real causes:

-   Manual data entry

-   Poor workflow

-   Duplicate cases

-   Missing information

-   System limitations

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What Root Cause Analysis is

-   Difference between problem, symptom, and root cause

-   RCA process

-   5 Whys technique

-   Fishbone Diagram (Ishikawa)

-   Pareto Analysis

-   Fault Tree Analysis

-   RCA documentation

-   Healthcare/pharma examples

**1. What is Root Cause Analysis (RCA)?**

Definition:

Root Cause Analysis is a structured approach used to identify the
fundamental reason behind a problem so that corrective actions solve the
issue permanently.

Simple formula:

Problem

↓

Symptoms

↓

Investigation

↓

Root Cause

↓

Corrective Action

**2. Why RCA Matters for a BA**

Without RCA:

Teams fix symptoms.

Example:

Problem:

Customer complaints increased.

Solution:

Hire more support staff.

But actual cause:

Product usability issue.

Result:

Problem continues.

A BA uses RCA to answer:

\"Why is this happening?\"

**3. Problem vs Symptom vs Root Cause**

Very important concept.

**Symptom**

What we observe.

Example:

\"Regulatory submissions are late.\"

**Problem**

The business issue.

Example:

\"Safety workflow is inefficient.\"

**Root Cause**

The actual reason.

Example:

\"Manual review process has no prioritization mechanism.\"

Diagram:

Root Cause

↑

Problem

↑

Symptom

**4. RCA Process**

A professional RCA follows:

Define Problem

↓

Collect Data

↓

Identify Possible Causes

↓

Analyze Causes

↓

Find Root Cause

↓

Implement Solution

↓

Monitor Results

**Step 1: Define the Problem**

A good problem statement includes:

-   What happened?

-   Where?

-   When?

-   How often?

-   Impact?

Weak:

\"Cases are slow.\"

Strong:

\"Adverse event case processing exceeds target timelines by 30% due to
delays during manual information extraction.\"

**Step 2: Collect Data**

Sources:

-   System logs

-   User interviews

-   Reports

-   Process observation

-   Metrics

Example:

Data collected:

Average processing time:

45 minutes/case

Target:

20 minutes/case

**Step 3: Identify Possible Causes**

Methods:

-   5 Whys

-   Fishbone Diagram

-   Brainstorming

-   Data analysis

**5. 5 Whys Technique**

One of the most famous RCA methods.

Created from:

Toyota Production System.

Purpose:

Move from surface problem to root cause.

**Example: AI Pharmacovigilance**

Problem:

Case processing is delayed.

**Why 1:**

Why are cases delayed?

Because reviewers spend too much time extracting information.

**Why 2:**

Why does extraction take too long?

Because information comes from multiple documents.

**Why 3:**

Why are multiple documents difficult?

Because data is not structured.

**Why 4:**

Why is data not structured?

Because reports come from different sources and formats.

**Why 5:**

Why are formats inconsistent?

Because there is no standardized intake process.

**Root Cause:**

Lack of standardized data intake process.

**6. Fishbone Diagram (Ishikawa)**

Also called:

Cause-and-Effect Diagram.

Used to organize possible causes.

Common categories:

**6M Model**

**1. Man (People)**

Issues:

-   Lack of training

-   Human error

**2. Machine (Technology)**

Issues:

-   Legacy systems

-   Poor tools

**3. Method (Process)**

Issues:

-   Inefficient workflow

-   Missing SOP

**4. Material (Data/Input)**

Issues:

-   Poor data quality

-   Missing information

**5. Measurement**

Issues:

-   Wrong metrics

-   No monitoring

**6. Environment**

Issues:

-   Organizational factors

**Example:**

Problem:

Delayed safety reports

Delayed Reports

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

People Process Technology

Training Manual Legacy System

Issue Workflow Limitations

Data Measurement

Missing No Tracking

Information Metrics

**7. Pareto Analysis (80/20 Rule)**

Principle:

80% of problems usually come from 20% of causes.

Used to prioritize causes.

Example:

100 complaints:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Cause**                                **Complaints**
  ---------------------------------------- ------------------------------
  Slow processing                          60

  Incorrect data                           25

  UI issue                                 10

  Other                                    5
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Focus first:

Slow processing.

**8. Fault Tree Analysis**

Used for complex systems.

Starts with:

Top failure event.

Then breaks down causes.

Example:

Top Event:

\"Incorrect safety report submitted\"

Possible causes:

Incorrect Submission

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Wrong Data Wrong Review

\|

Missing Information

**9. RCA vs Gap Analysis**

Important interview question.

**Gap Analysis**

Question:

\"What is missing?\"

Example:

No AI capability.

**RCA**

Question:

\"Why is the problem happening?\"

Example:

Manual workflow exists because legacy systems were not upgraded.

They work together.

Example:

RCA finds:

Manual extraction is the cause.

Gap Analysis finds:

AI extraction capability is missing.

**10. RCA Documentation Template**

A professional RCA document:

**1. Problem Statement**

Example:

\"Case processing exceeds regulatory timeline.\"

**2. Business Impact**

Example:

-   Increased workload

-   Compliance risk

**3. Data Analysis**

Evidence:

Processing time reports.

**4. Root Cause Identification**

Methods:

-   5 Whys

-   Fishbone

**5. Root Cause**

Example:

Manual information extraction.

**6. Corrective Action**

Example:

Implement AI-assisted extraction.

**7. Preventive Action**

Example:

Standardize intake process.

**11. Healthcare Example: Medication Error RCA**

Problem:

Wrong medication dosage given.

Symptoms:

Patient received incorrect dose.

5 Whys:

Why?

Doctor entered wrong dosage.

Why?

Selection list was confusing.

Why?

Similar drug names displayed together.

Why?

No medication safety design review.

Why?

UX validation was not part of development process.

Root Cause:

Poor medication interface design.

Solution:

Improve UX + validation workflow.

**12. RCA in Agile**

RCA is used during:

**Sprint Retrospectives**

Example:

Problem:

Sprint missed deadline.

Team asks:

Why?

Root cause:

Requirements were unclear.

Action:

Improve refinement process.

**Production Issues**

Example:

Feature failure.

RCA identifies:

Technical/process cause.

**13. BA Interview Questions**

**Q1: What is RCA?**

Answer:

\"Root Cause Analysis is a structured method used to identify the
underlying reason behind a problem rather than only addressing
symptoms.\"

**Q2: Which RCA techniques have you used?**

Answer:

\"I have used techniques such as 5 Whys, Fishbone Diagram, and Pareto
Analysis to identify root causes and prioritize corrective actions.\"

**Q3: Give a healthcare example.**

Answer:

\"For delayed pharmacovigilance processing, I would analyze workflow,
data, technology, and people factors to identify whether the root cause
is manual processes, poor data quality, or system limitations.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create an RCA document for:

**Problem:**

\"Pharmacovigilance case processing is delayed.\"

Include:

1.  Problem statement

2.  Impact

3.  Data/evidence

4.  5 Whys analysis

5.  Fishbone causes

6.  Root cause

7.  Corrective action

8.  Preventive action

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ What RCA is\
✅ Problem vs symptom vs root cause\
✅ RCA process\
✅ 5 Whys\
✅ Fishbone Diagram\
✅ Pareto Analysis\
✅ Fault Tree Analysis\
✅ RCA documentation\
✅ Healthcare examples

**Lesson 7: Impact Analysis Masterclass**

**A BA does not only ask \"What should we change?\"**

A senior BA asks:

**\"If we make this change, what else will be affected?\"**

This thinking prevents unexpected failures.

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What Impact Analysis is

-   Why BAs perform Impact Analysis

-   Types of impacts

-   Change Impact Assessment

-   Dependency Analysis

-   Risk Impact Analysis

-   Business, technical, and user impact

-   Impact Analysis document creation

-   Healthcare AI example

**1. What is Impact Analysis?**

Definition:

Impact Analysis is a technique used to identify the consequences,
dependencies, risks, and affected areas of a proposed change.

Simple formula:

Change Request

↓

What will be affected?

↓

Impact Assessment

↓

Decision

↓

Implementation Plan

**2. Why Impact Analysis Matters**

Without Impact Analysis:

A small change can create unexpected problems.

Example:

Business request:

\"Add AI-generated medical summary.\"

Looks simple.

But impact includes:

-   Data privacy review

-   AI validation

-   User training

-   Regulatory approval

-   System integration

**3. When Do BAs Perform Impact Analysis?**

Impact Analysis is performed during:

**Requirement Changes**

Example:

New feature request.

**System Changes**

Example:

API modification.

**Process Changes**

Example:

New approval workflow.

**Regulatory Changes**

Example:

New compliance requirement.

**Defect Fixes**

Example:

Changing existing behavior.

**4. Impact Analysis Process**

A BA follows:

Understand Change

↓

Identify Affected Areas

↓

Analyze Impact

↓

Assess Risk

↓

Estimate Effort

↓

Recommend Action

**Step 1: Understand the Change**

Example:

Change:

\"AI should automatically classify adverse events.\"

Questions:

-   Why is this needed?

-   Who requested it?

-   What problem does it solve?

**Step 2: Identify Affected Areas**

A BA checks:

**Business**

Will business goals change?

**Process**

Will workflow change?

**Users**

Will users need training?

**Technology**

Will systems change?

**Data**

Will data requirements change?

**Compliance**

Will regulations be affected?

**5. Types of Impact Analysis**

**1. Business Impact Analysis**

Focus:

Business operations.

Questions:

-   Does revenue change?

-   Does cost change?

-   Does process change?

Example:

AI automation reduces manual review effort.

Impact:

Positive:

Lower operational cost.

**2. User Impact Analysis**

Focus:

People using the system.

Questions:

-   Will user workflow change?

-   Is training needed?

-   Will adoption be affected?

Example:

Reviewer moves from manual writing to AI-assisted review.

Impact:

Need AI training.

**3. Technical Impact Analysis**

Focus:

Technology changes.

Questions:

-   Which systems are affected?

-   Are APIs impacted?

-   Is database change required?

Example:

Adding RAG-based AI assistant.

Impact:

Need:

-   Vector database

-   AI services

-   Security review

**4. Data Impact Analysis**

Focus:

Information changes.

Questions:

-   What data is required?

-   How is data stored?

-   Is privacy affected?

Example:

AI needs historical adverse event cases.

Impact:

Need:

-   Data cleaning

-   Access approval

**5. Process Impact Analysis**

Focus:

Workflow changes.

Example:

Current:

Reviewer writes narrative manually.

Future:

AI generates draft.

Impact:

Approval workflow changes.

**6. Compliance Impact Analysis**

Very important in healthcare.

Questions:

-   Does regulation apply?

-   Is validation required?

-   Is audit trail needed?

Example:

AI recommendation affects safety decisions.

Impact:

Need:

-   Human oversight

-   Documentation

-   Validation

**6. Impact Analysis Matrix**

A common BA artifact.

Example:

Change:

AI Case Summary Feature

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Area**     **Impact**                  **Severity**   **Action**
  ------------ --------------------------- -------------- ---------------------
  Process      Review workflow changes     High           Update SOP

  Users        New AI workflow             Medium         Training

  Technology   Add AI service              High           Architecture review

  Data         Need historical cases       High           Data preparation

  Compliance   AI validation needed        High           Regulatory review
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

**7. Dependency Analysis**

A change rarely exists alone.

BA identifies dependencies.

Example:

Feature:

AI medical coding suggestion.

Dependencies:

AI Coding Feature

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Drug Database

Medical Terms

User Interface

Approval Workflow

If dependency fails:

Feature fails.

**8. Impact Analysis Example: Pharmacovigilance AI**

**Change Request:**

\"Allow AI to generate safety narratives.\"

**Business Impact**

Positive:

-   Faster processing

-   Reduced workload

**User Impact**

Need:

-   Reviewer training

-   New workflow adoption

**Technical Impact**

Need:

-   LLM integration

-   Document processing pipeline

**Data Impact**

Need:

-   Historical cases

-   Regulatory documents

**Compliance Impact**

Need:

-   Audit trail

-   Human approval

**9. Impact Analysis Document Structure**

Professional BA format:

**1. Change Description**

What is changing?

Example:

\"Introduce AI-generated case narratives.\"

**2. Reason for Change**

Why needed?

Example:

Reduce manual documentation effort.

**3. Impact Areas**

Analyze:

-   Business

-   Process

-   User

-   Technology

-   Data

-   Compliance

**4. Risk Assessment**

Identify:

-   Risks

-   Probability

-   Mitigation

**5. Recommendation**

Decision:

Approve / Reject / Modify

**10. Impact Analysis vs Gap Analysis vs RCA**

Very important distinction.

**Gap Analysis**

Question:

\"What is missing?\"

Example:

No AI capability exists.

**RCA**

Question:

\"Why does the problem exist?\"

Example:

Manual workflow causes delays.

**Impact Analysis**

Question:

\"What happens if we change something?\"

Example:

Adding AI affects workflow, users, compliance.

Together:

RCA

↓

Find cause

↓

Gap Analysis

↓

Find missing capability

↓

Impact Analysis

↓

Understand change consequences

**11. Impact Analysis in Agile**

Agile changes frequently.

Before adding a backlog item:

BA/Product Owner checks:

-   Business value

-   Dependencies

-   Risks

-   Effort

Example:

New requirement:

\"Add AI chatbot.\"

Impact:

-   New UI

-   New AI model

-   Security review

-   Additional testing

**12. Interview Questions**

**Q1: What is Impact Analysis?**

Answer:

\"Impact Analysis evaluates the consequences of a proposed change by
identifying affected processes, systems, users, data, risks, and
dependencies.\"

**Q2: How do you perform Impact Analysis?**

Answer:

\"I first understand the change, identify impacted areas, analyze risks
and dependencies, estimate effort, and recommend the best approach.\"

**Q3: Give an example.**

Answer:

\"When introducing an AI feature in pharmacovigilance, I would assess
impact on workflow, users, technology architecture, data privacy, and
regulatory compliance.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create an **Impact Analysis Document**:

**Change:**

\"Implement AI-generated adverse event summaries.\"

Analyze:

1.  Change description

2.  Business impact

3.  User impact

4.  Process impact

5.  Technical impact

6.  Data impact

7.  Compliance impact

8.  Risks

9.  Recommendation

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ What Impact Analysis is\
✅ Why BAs use it\
✅ Impact categories\
✅ Dependency analysis\
✅ Change impact assessment\
✅ Impact Analysis documentation\
✅ Healthcare AI example\
✅ Difference between RCA, Gap Analysis, and Impact Analysis

**Lesson 8: Decision Analysis & Decision Matrix Masterclass**

**A BA is not only a requirement collector. A senior BA helps
organizations make better decisions.**

In real projects, stakeholders rarely agree immediately.

Examples:

-   Should we build AI internally or buy a solution?

-   Should we prioritize feature A or feature B?

-   Should we fix the current system or replace it?

-   Which requirement gives maximum business value?

A BA uses **Decision Analysis techniques** to make decisions based on
facts, not opinions.

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What Decision Analysis is

-   Why BAs use decision techniques

-   Decision-making framework

-   Decision Matrix

-   Weighted Decision Matrix

-   Cost-Benefit Analysis

-   Risk-Based Decision Making

-   Trade-off Analysis

-   Healthcare AI examples

**1. What is Decision Analysis?**

Definition:

Decision Analysis is a structured approach used to evaluate different
options and select the best solution based on business objectives,
constraints, risks, and value.

Simple formula:

Problem

↓

Possible Options

↓

Evaluate Criteria

↓

Compare Alternatives

↓

Select Best Decision

**2. Why Decision Analysis Matters for a BA**

Without structured analysis:

Decision becomes:

-   Based on personal preference

-   Influenced by seniority

-   Emotion-driven

Example:

Executive says:

\"Let\'s buy an AI tool.\"

Developer says:

\"Let\'s build ourselves.\"

Who is correct?

A BA analyzes:

-   Cost

-   Time

-   Risk

-   Capability

-   Business value

**3. Decision Analysis Process**

A BA follows:

Define Decision

↓

Identify Options

↓

Define Evaluation Criteria

↓

Collect Data

↓

Score Options

↓

Recommend Decision

↓

Document Decision

**Step 1: Define Decision**

Example:

Decision:

\"How should we implement AI pharmacovigilance capability?\"

**Step 2: Identify Options**

Possible choices:

Option A:

Build AI solution internally.

Option B:

Buy commercial AI platform.

Option C:

Hybrid approach.

**Step 3: Define Criteria**

Criteria may include:

-   Cost

-   Time

-   Quality

-   Risk

-   Scalability

-   Compliance

**4. Decision Matrix**

A Decision Matrix compares options using criteria.

Example:

Decision:

Select AI implementation approach.

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Criteria**           **Build**        **Buy**          **Hybrid**
  ---------------------- ---------------- ---------------- ----------------
  Cost                   Medium           High             Medium

  Speed                  Slow             Fast             Medium

  Control                High             Low              High

  Compliance             High             Medium           High
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------

Problem:

Not all criteria have equal importance.

Solution:

Weighted Decision Matrix.

**5. Weighted Decision Matrix**

This is a very common BA technique.

Formula:

Score = Weight × Rating

Example:

Selecting AI Solution.

Criteria:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Criteria**                                **Weight**
  ------------------------------------------- ---------------------------
  Compliance                                  40%

  Cost                                        20%

  Speed                                       20%

  Scalability                                 20%
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Rating:

1 = Poor

5 = Excellent

**Option A: Build AI**

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Criteria**               **Weight**       **Rating**     **Score**
  -------------------------- ---------------- -------------- -------------
  Compliance                 40               5              200

  Cost                       20               3              60

  Speed                      20               2              40

  Scalability                20               5              100
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

Total:

400

**Option B: Buy AI**

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Criteria**               **Weight**       **Rating**     **Score**
  -------------------------- ---------------- -------------- -------------
  Compliance                 40               3              120

  Cost                       20               2              40

  Speed                      20               5              100

  Scalability                20               4              80
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

Total:

340

Decision:

Build wins.

**6. Cost-Benefit Analysis**

Used to compare:

Cost vs Expected Value.

Formula:

ROI = (Benefit - Cost) / Cost

Example:

AI Implementation

Investment:

₹1 crore

Expected yearly benefit:

₹2 crore

ROI:

(2 - 1) / 1

= 100%

**7. Risk-Based Decision Analysis**

Some decisions have higher risks.

BA evaluates:

**Risk Probability**

How likely?

**Risk Impact**

How serious?

Risk Score:

Probability × Impact

Example:

AI Auto Approval Feature

Probability:

High

Impact:

Very High

Risk:

Critical

Decision:

Require human approval.

**8. Trade-off Analysis**

Real projects always involve trade-offs.

Example:

You cannot maximize:

-   Speed

-   Cost

-   Quality

simultaneously.

Common trade-offs:

**Speed vs Quality**

Fast delivery may reduce testing time.

**Cost vs Features**

More features increase cost.

**Automation vs Control**

More automation may reduce human oversight.

**Healthcare AI Example**

**Decision:**

Should AI automatically approve safety cases?

Option 1:

Full automation

Benefits:

-   Faster processing

Risks:

-   Compliance risk

-   Wrong decisions

Option 2:

AI Assistance + Human Approval

Benefits:

-   Faster

-   Safer

Risks:

-   Some manual effort remains

Decision:

Hybrid approach.

Why?

Best balance:

Speed + Safety.

**9. Decision Log**

A professional BA documents decisions.

Template:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Field**                       **Example**
  ------------------------------- ---------------------------------------
  Decision                        Use AI assistance model

  Date                            Jan 2026

  Options considered              Build, Buy, Hybrid

  Criteria                        Cost, Risk, Compliance

  Decision owner                  Product Manager

  Reason                          Best business value
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**10. Decision Analysis in Agile**

Agile teams make continuous decisions.

Examples:

-   Which backlog item first?

-   Which feature enters MVP?

-   Which defect gets priority?

Techniques used:

-   Weighted scoring

-   MoSCoW prioritization

-   Cost of delay

-   Business value scoring

**11. Decision Analysis vs Prioritization**

Important distinction.

**Decision Analysis**

Chooses between alternatives.

Example:

Build vs Buy.

**Prioritization**

Chooses order.

Example:

Feature A before Feature B.

**12. BA Interview Questions**

**Q1: How do you support decision making?**

Answer:

\"I use structured techniques such as decision matrices, cost-benefit
analysis, and risk assessment to compare options and recommend decisions
based on business objectives.\"

**Q2: What is a weighted decision matrix?**

Answer:

\"A weighted decision matrix assigns importance weights to evaluation
criteria and scores each option to identify the best alternative
objectively.\"

**Q3: Give a healthcare example.**

Answer:

\"For implementing AI in pharmacovigilance, I would compare build, buy,
and hybrid approaches using criteria such as compliance, cost,
scalability, and implementation risk.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a Decision Matrix:

**Decision:**

\"How should AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot be implemented?\"

Options:

1.  Build internally

2.  Buy vendor solution

3.  Hybrid approach

Criteria:

-   Cost

-   Speed

-   Compliance

-   Scalability

-   Control

Create:

-   Weight

-   Rating

-   Final score

-   Recommendation

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ What Decision Analysis is\
✅ Decision-making framework\
✅ Decision Matrix\
✅ Weighted Decision Matrix\
✅ Cost-Benefit Analysis\
✅ Risk-based decisions\
✅ Trade-off analysis\
✅ Decision documentation\
✅ Healthcare AI examples

**Lesson 9: Business Analysis Frameworks -- SWOT, PESTLE & Strategic
Analysis Toolkit**

**A BA does not analyze only requirements. A senior BA understands the
business environment in which the product operates.**

Before creating solutions, a BA should understand:

-   Internal strengths and weaknesses

-   External opportunities and threats

-   Market conditions

-   Regulations

-   Business capabilities

-   Competitive environment

These frameworks help answer:

\"Should we do this? Why now? What risks exist? How can we succeed?\"

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   SWOT Analysis

-   PESTLE Analysis

-   Porter's Five Forces

-   Value Chain Analysis

-   Business Capability Analysis

-   When to use each framework

-   Healthcare AI example

**1. SWOT Analysis**

**What is SWOT?**

SWOT stands for:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Letter**             **Meaning**
  ---------------------- ------------------------------------------------
  S                      Strengths

  W                      Weaknesses

  O                      Opportunities

  T                      Threats
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

It analyzes:

-   Internal factors

-   External factors

**SWOT Structure**

SWOT

INTERNAL EXTERNAL

Strengths Opportunities

Weaknesses Threats

**2. Strengths Analysis**

Strengths are internal advantages.

Questions:

-   What do we do well?

-   What resources do we have?

-   What makes us different?

**Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

Strengths:

✅ Strong pharma domain expertise\
✅ Existing safety databases\
✅ Experienced medical reviewers\
✅ Regulatory knowledge

**3. Weakness Analysis**

Weaknesses are internal limitations.

Questions:

-   What are we missing?

-   Where are we inefficient?

Example:

Weaknesses:

❌ Manual processes\
❌ Poor data standardization\
❌ Limited AI skills\
❌ Legacy systems

**4. Opportunities Analysis**

Opportunities are external possibilities.

Examples:

-   AI adoption increasing

-   Healthcare automation demand

-   Regulatory technology growth

-   Digital transformation

**5. Threat Analysis**

Threats are external risks.

Examples:

-   Regulatory restrictions

-   Data privacy concerns

-   Competitor solutions

-   AI trust issues

**SWOT Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Strengths**                       **Weaknesses**
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Pharma expertise                    Legacy systems

  Domain knowledge                    Limited AI maturity
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Opportunities**            **Threats**
  ---------------------------- ------------------------------------------
  AI automation                Regulatory uncertainty

  Cost reduction               Data privacy risks
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**When BA Uses SWOT?**

Useful during:

-   Product discovery

-   Business case creation

-   Strategic planning

-   New product evaluation

**6. PESTLE Analysis**

PESTLE analyzes external environment.

Meaning:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Letter**            **Factor**
  --------------------- -------------------------------------------------
  P                     Political

  E                     Economic

  S                     Social

  T                     Technological

  L                     Legal

  E                     Environmental
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**PESTLE Example: Healthcare AI Product**

**Political**

Government policies.

Example:

Healthcare AI regulations.

**Economic**

Financial factors.

Example:

Cost savings from automation.

**Social**

User acceptance.

Example:

Do doctors trust AI?

**Technological**

Technology trends.

Example:

LLMs, cloud computing, AI platforms.

**Legal**

Compliance requirements.

Example:

Patient data protection.

**Environmental**

Sustainability factors.

Example:

Cloud energy consumption.

**PESTLE Table**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Factor**                **Impact**
  ------------------------- ---------------------------------------------
  Political                 AI regulations

  Economic                  Cost reduction pressure

  Social                    Trust in AI

  Technology                Rapid AI innovation

  Legal                     Data privacy

  Environment               Sustainable IT
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**When BA Uses PESTLE?**

Useful for:

-   Market analysis

-   Product strategy

-   Enterprise projects

-   Regulatory industries

**7. Porter's Five Forces**

Used to analyze competitive environment.

Five forces:

New Entrants

↑

Suppliers ← Competition → Customers

↓

Substitute Products

**1. Competitive Rivalry**

Question:

How many competitors exist?

Healthcare AI example:

Many AI healthcare startups.

**2. Threat of New Entrants**

Question:

How easy is it for new companies to enter?

Example:

AI tools are becoming easier to build.

**3. Supplier Power**

Suppliers:

-   Cloud providers

-   AI model providers

-   Data providers

**4. Buyer Power**

Customers:

-   Pharma companies

-   Hospitals

**5. Substitute Threat**

Alternative solutions:

-   Manual processes

-   Traditional software

**8. Value Chain Analysis**

Used to understand where value is created.

Michael Porter model.

Primary activities:

Input

↓

Operations

↓

Delivery

↓

Marketing

↓

Support

Healthcare Example:

Drug Safety Value Chain:

Case Intake

↓

Medical Review

↓

Risk Assessment

↓

Regulatory Reporting

↓

Safety Monitoring

BA identifies:

Where can improvement create maximum value?

**9. Business Capability Analysis**

Very important for senior BAs.

A capability describes:

\"What the business must be able to do.\"

Example:

Business Capability:

\"Adverse Event Management\"

Current capability:

Manual processing.

Future capability:

AI-assisted safety management.

Capability Map:

Pharmacovigilance Capability

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Case Intake

Case Review

Risk Analysis

Reporting

Monitoring

**10. Framework Selection Guide**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Need**                                        **Framework**
  ----------------------------------------------- -----------------------
  Understand internal/external situation          SWOT

  Analyze market environment                      PESTLE

  Study competition                               Porter Five Forces

  Find business value areas                       Value Chain

  Understand business functions                   Capability Analysis
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**11. Combining Frameworks**

A senior BA often combines methods.

Example:

Launching AI Pharmacovigilance Product:

**SWOT**

Understand internal readiness.

**PESTLE**

Understand regulations.

**Capability Analysis**

Understand required business abilities.

**Gap Analysis**

Find missing capabilities.

**Impact Analysis**

Understand change effects.

**Complete BA Strategic Analysis Flow**

Business Strategy

↓

SWOT / PESTLE

↓

Capability Analysis

↓

Gap Analysis

↓

RCA

↓

Impact Analysis

↓

Requirements

**12. Interview Questions**

**Q1: What is SWOT Analysis?**

Answer:

\"SWOT Analysis identifies internal strengths and weaknesses and
external opportunities and threats to support business
decision-making.\"

**Q2: Difference between SWOT and PESTLE?**

Answer:

\"SWOT focuses on internal and external business factors, while PESTLE
focuses mainly on external environmental factors affecting the
organization.\"

**Q3: Why would a BA use these frameworks?**

Answer:

\"They help understand business context, identify risks, evaluate
opportunities, and ensure solutions align with organizational
strategy.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Perform strategic analysis for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot**

Create:

**1. SWOT Analysis**

-   Strengths

-   Weaknesses

-   Opportunities

-   Threats

**2. PESTLE Analysis**

Analyze:

-   Political

-   Economic

-   Social

-   Technology

-   Legal

-   Environmental

**3. Capability Map**

Identify:

Current vs Future capabilities.

**Lesson Summary**

You learned:

✅ SWOT Analysis\
✅ PESTLE Analysis\
✅ Porter's Five Forces\
✅ Value Chain Analysis\
✅ Business Capability Analysis\
✅ Framework selection\
✅ Strategic BA thinking

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1 -- Modern BA Role\
✅ Lesson 2 -- Business Problem Identification\
✅ Lesson 3 -- Stakeholder Management\
