**PHASE 5 -- Process Analysis, Modeling & Solution Design**

**Lesson 31: Process Modeling Masterclass**

**BPMN, UML, Workflow Design & AS-IS / TO-BE Modeling**

**A Business Analyst does not only document requirements. A BA
visualizes how work happens today and how it should happen after
improvement.**

Before building any system, the team needs to understand:

-   What is the current process?

-   Who performs each activity?

-   Where are delays?

-   What should the improved process look like?

Process modeling helps convert messy business activities into clear
visual workflows.

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What is process modeling

-   Why BAs use process models

-   AS-IS vs TO-BE analysis

-   BPMN fundamentals

-   BPMN symbols

-   Swimlane diagrams

-   UML diagrams

-   Workflow design

-   Healthcare AI process example

**1. What is Process Modeling?**

Definition:

Process modeling is the visual representation of business activities,
decisions, actors, inputs, and outputs involved in completing a business
process.

Simple:

It shows:

\"Who does what, when, and how?\"

Example:

Current Pharmacovigilance Process:

Receive Report

↓

Read Document

↓

Extract Information

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submit Report

A process model makes this easier to understand.

**2. Why Does a BA Need Process Modeling?**

A BA uses process models to:

✅ Understand current problems\
✅ Identify inefficiencies\
✅ Find automation opportunities\
✅ Explain workflows to stakeholders\
✅ Define system requirements\
✅ Design future processes

Example:

Stakeholder says:

\"Our case review process is slow.\"

BA creates process model.

Finds:

70% time spent manually entering data.

Solution:

AI extraction.

**3. AS-IS vs TO-BE Process**

One of the most important BA concepts.

**AS-IS Process**

Meaning:

Current state.

Question:

\"How does work happen today?\"

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance:

Case Received

↓

Reviewer Opens PDF

↓

Manual Data Entry

↓

Manual Classification

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submission

Problems:

-   Manual work

-   Errors

-   Slow processing

**TO-BE Process**

Meaning:

Future improved state.

Question:

\"How should work happen?\"

Example:

Case Received

↓

AI Extracts Information

↓

AI Generates Summary

↓

Reviewer Validates

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submission

Benefits:

-   Faster

-   More consistent

-   Less manual effort

**4. Gap Between AS-IS and TO-BE**

BA identifies:

\"What needs to change?\"

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Area**         **AS-IS**    **TO-BE**           **Improvement**
  ---------------- ------------ ------------------- ---------------------
  Data Entry       Manual       AI extraction       Automation

  Review           Long         Prioritized         Faster

  Reporting        Manual       Automated           Efficiency
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**5. Introduction to BPMN**

BPMN:

**Business Process Model and Notation**

Definition:

BPMN is a standardized graphical language used to model business
processes.

Used by:

-   Business Analysts

-   Process Architects

-   Product Teams

-   Developers

BPMN helps everyone understand the same process.

**6. Core BPMN Elements**

BPMN has four main categories:

Events

↓

Activities

↓

Gateways

↓

Connections

**7. BPMN Event**

An event represents something that happens.

Symbol:

⭕ Circle

Types:

**Start Event**

Process begins.

Example:

\"Case Received\"

**End Event**

Process finishes.

Example:

\"Case Submitted\"

**Intermediate Event**

Something happens during process.

Example:

\"Approval received\"

**8. BPMN Activity / Task**

Activity represents work performed.

Symbol:

Rounded rectangle

Examples:

Upload Document

Generate AI Summary

Review Case

Approve Report

**9. BPMN Gateway**

Gateway represents decision points.

Symbol:

◇ Diamond

Example:

AI Analysis Complete

\|

\|

Risk Level?

/ \\

High Low

\| \|

Manual Auto

Review Continue

**10. BPMN Sequence Flow**

Shows process order.

Symbol:

→

Example:

Upload File

↓

AI Processing

↓

Review

**11. Swimlane Diagram**

Very important BA technique.

A swimlane shows:

-   Who performs activity

-   Responsibility boundaries

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Process:

Safety Reviewer AI System Medical Reviewer

Receive Case

↓

Extract Data

↓

Generate Summary

Review Output

↓

Approve

**Why Swimlanes Matter?**

They identify:

-   Ownership

-   Handoffs

-   Delays

-   Automation opportunities

**12. BPMN Example: AI Case Review Process**

**AS-IS**

Start

↓

Receive Report

↓

Reviewer Reads Document

↓

Manual Data Entry

↓

Manual Risk Assessment

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submit

↓

End

Problems:

1.  Too much manual effort

2.  Human errors

3.  Slow turnaround

**TO-BE**

Start

↓

Upload Report

↓

AI Extracts Information

↓

AI Generates Summary

↓

AI Calculates Risk

↓

Reviewer Validates

↓

Medical Approval

↓

Submit

↓

End

**13. UML for Business Analysts**

UML:

**Unified Modeling Language**

Definition:

UML is a standardized modeling language used to describe system behavior
and structure.

BAs commonly use:

1.  Use Case Diagram

2.  Activity Diagram

3.  Sequence Diagram

4.  Class Diagram

**14. UML Use Case Diagram**

Shows:

Who interacts with system?

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance System

Actors:

-   Safety Reviewer

-   Medical Reviewer

-   Administrator

Use Cases:

-   Upload Case

-   Review AI Summary

-   Approve Case

-   Generate Report

Visual:

Safety Reviewer

\|

\|

Upload Case

\|

\|

AI System

\|

\|

Generate Summary

**15. UML Activity Diagram**

Similar to workflow.

Shows:

-   Actions

-   Decisions

-   Flow

Example:

Start

↓

Upload Document

↓

Validate File

↓

\[Valid?\]

Yes → Process

No → Error Message

↓

End

**16. UML Sequence Diagram**

Shows interaction between systems.

Example:

AI Summary Generation:

Reviewer

\|

\| Upload Document

↓

Application

\|

\| Send Document

↓

AI Engine

\|

\| Return Summary

↓

Application

\|

↓

Reviewer

**17. BPMN vs UML**

Important interview question.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **BPMN**                       **UML**
  ------------------------------ ----------------------------------------
  Business process               System behavior

  Focus on workflow              Focus on software design

  Business users                 Technical teams

  Shows activities               Shows interactions
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**18. Workflow Design Principles**

A good workflow should have:

**Clear Start and End**

Avoid unclear processes.

**Defined Ownership**

Every activity has responsible person/system.

**Decision Points**

Define rules.

**Exception Handling**

What happens when things fail?

Example:

AI confidence \< 70%

↓

Manual review

**19. Real Healthcare AI Example**

**Project:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

BA creates:

**AS-IS Model**

Shows:

Manual case processing.

**Problems Identified:**

-   Duplicate work

-   Delays

-   Manual classification

**TO-BE Model**

Introduces:

-   AI extraction

-   AI summary

-   Risk scoring

**Requirements Generated:**

User Stories:

\"As a reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can review
cases faster.\"

**20. Interview Questions**

**Q1: Why do BAs create process models?**

Answer:

\"Process models help BAs understand current workflows, identify
improvements, communicate requirements, and design future-state
solutions.\"

**Q2: Difference between AS-IS and TO-BE?**

Answer:

\"AS-IS represents the current process, while TO-BE represents the
desired improved process after changes.\"

**Q3: BPMN vs UML?**

Answer:

\"BPMN focuses on business processes and workflows, while UML focuses on
system behavior and software modeling.\"

For AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot:

Include:

-   Roles involved

-   Problems

**2. TO-BE Process**

Include:

-   AI automation points

-   Human review points

-   Decision points

**3. BPMN Elements**

Identify:

-   Start Event

-   Tasks

-   Gateway

-   End Event

**4. UML Use Case**

Actors:

-   Safety Reviewer

-   Medical Reviewer

-   Administrator

Use cases:

Minimum 5

You learned:

**Lesson 32: Advanced BPMN 2.0 Masterclass**

**Professional Business Process Modeling for Senior Business Analysts**

**1. Why Advanced BPMN Matters for a BA**

A beginner BA creates simple flowcharts:

Start

\|

Task

\|

Task

\|

End

A senior BA models enterprise processes where:

-   Multiple teams are involved

-   Systems communicate

-   Decisions happen

-   Exceptions occur

-   Parallel activities run together

-   Compliance steps exist

Example:

A healthcare AI platform:

Patient Report

\|

↓

Pharmacovigilance System

\|

↓

AI Engine

\|

↓

Safety Reviewer

\|

↓

Medical Approval

\|

↓

Regulatory Submission

This requires BPMN.

**2. What is BPMN 2.0?**

BPMN:

**Business Process Model and Notation**

BPMN 2.0 is the international standard for representing business
processes visually.

It helps communication between:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Team**                **Use**
  ----------------------- -----------------------------------------------
  Business                Understand workflow

  BA                      Analyze requirements

  Developers              Understand system behavior

  QA                      Create test scenarios

  Management              Understand operations
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**3. BPMN Core Components**

BPMN has 5 major elements:

1\. Flow Objects

2\. Connecting Objects

3\. Swimlanes

4\. Artifacts

5\. Data Objects

**PART 1: Flow Objects**

Flow objects represent what happens in a process.

There are three:

1.  Events

2.  Activities

3.  Gateways

**4. Events in BPMN**

Events represent something that happens.

Symbol:

Circle

There are three categories:

Start Event

Intermediate Event

End Event

**5. Start Events**

Start events begin a process.

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance:

○

New Adverse Event Report Received

Meaning:

The process starts when a report arrives.

Types of Start Events:

**None Start Event**

Normal beginning.

Example:

\"User submits form\"

**Message Start Event**

Triggered by message.

Example:

Drug Safety Report Received

**Timer Start Event**

Triggered by time.

Example:

Every Monday generate compliance report

**Conditional Start Event**

Triggered by condition.

Example:

When pending cases \> 500

**6. End Events**

End the process.

Example:

◎

Report Submitted Successfully

Types:

**Normal End**

Process completed.

**Message End**

Send information.

Example:

Send report to regulator.

**Error End**

Process stopped due to failure.

Example:

AI processing failed.

**7. Intermediate Events**

Happen during the process.

Example:

Receive Case

↓

AI Processing

↓

Wait for Approval

↓

Submit

\"Wait for Approval\" is an intermediate event.

Common Intermediate Events:

**Timer Event**

Wait for time.

Example:

Wait 24 hours for response.

**Message Event**

Receive/send information.

Example:

Receive approval email.

**Error Event**

Handle failure.

Example:

AI model unavailable.

**8. Activities in BPMN**

Activities represent work.

Two types:

Task

Sub-process

**9. BPMN Task**

A single activity.

Example:

□

Review AI Summary

Common Task Types:

**User Task**

Performed by human.

Example:

Safety reviewer validates case.

**Service Task**

Performed automatically by system.

Example:

AI extracts drug information.

**Manual Task**

Outside system.

Example:

Phone verification.

**Script Task**

Automated logic.

Example:

Calculate risk score.

**10. BPMN Sub-Process**

A group of activities.

Example:

Instead of:

Review Case

↓

Check Drug

↓

Check Patient

↓

Check Severity

↓

Approve

Create:

Review Case

\|

↓

\[Sub Process\]

Medical Review

Benefits:

-   Cleaner diagrams

-   Easier maintenance

-   Better understanding

**11. Gateways (Decision Logic)**

Gateway controls process flow.

Symbol:

Diamond ◇

Used for:

-   Decisions

-   Branching

-   Parallel activities

**Type 1: Exclusive Gateway (XOR)**

Only one path.

Example:

High Risk?

◇

/ \\

Yes No

\| \|

Manual Review Auto Continue

**Type 2: Parallel Gateway (AND)**

Multiple paths happen together.

Example:

After case submission:

◇

/ \\

Generate Report Notify Team

\\ /

Continue

Both happen.

**Type 3: Inclusive Gateway (OR)**

One or more paths.

Example:

Additional reviews:

Need Review?

Clinical

Regulatory

Legal

**12. Connecting Objects**

These connect process elements.

Three types:

**Sequence Flow**

Shows order.

Example:

Task A → Task B

**Message Flow**

Communication between participants.

Example:

AI System

\~\~\~\~\~\>

Regulatory System

**Association**

Links information.

Example:

Attach document to task.

**13. Pools and Lanes**

Very important senior BA concept.

**Pool**

Represents an organization or participant.

Example:

Pool:

Pharmaceutical Company

**Lane**

Represents role/team.

Example:

Pharma Company

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Safety Team

Medical Team

Regulatory Team

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

**Example: AI Pharmacovigilance BPMN**

Pool:

Pharma Company

Lanes:

1.  Safety Reviewer

2.  AI System

3.  Medical Reviewer

Process:

Safety Reviewer

(Start)

Receive Case

↓

AI System

Extract Data

Generate Summary

↓

Safety Reviewer

Validate Output

↓

Gateway

Risk?

High Low

\| \|

Medical Review Approve

↓

Regulatory Team

Submit Report

(End)

**14. Data Objects in BPMN**

Represent information used by activities.

Examples:

Documents:

-   Patient report

-   Medical history

-   Drug information

-   AI summary

Example:

Upload Report

\|

\[Case Document\]

**15. BPMN Best Practices for BAs**

**Rule 1**

One activity = one action

Bad:

\"Review and Approve Case\"

Good:

\"Review Case\"

\"Approve Case\"

**Rule 2**

Always define ownership.

Who performs this?

**Rule 3**

Model exceptions.

Example:

What if AI fails?

**Rule 4**

Keep AS-IS and TO-BE separate.

Do not mix current and future states.

**16. Real Project Example**

**Problem:**

Case processing takes 5 days.

BA creates AS-IS BPMN:

Finds:

-   Manual extraction

-   Duplicate review

-   Approval delays

TO-BE BPMN:

Adds:

-   AI extraction

-   Automated classification

-   Notification workflow

Result:

Processing reduced to 2 days.

**Interview Questions**

**Q1. Why use BPMN?**

Answer:

\"BPMN provides a standardized way to visualize business processes,
making communication easier between business stakeholders and technical
teams.\"

**Q2. Difference between Task and Sub-process?**

Answer:

\"A task represents a single activity, while a sub-process represents a
collection of related activities.\"

**Q3. Difference between Sequence Flow and Message Flow?**

Answer:

\"Sequence flow shows the order of activities inside a process, while
message flow represents communication between different participants.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create:

**AS-IS BPMN**

Include:

-   3 pools/lanes

-   10 activities

-   2 decisions

-   1 exception path

**TO-BE BPMN**

Add:

-   AI extraction

-   AI risk scoring

-   Human validation

-   Automated notification

Today you learned:

**Lesson 33: Workflow Design Masterclass**

**Designing Efficient Business Operations & Digital Workflows**

**A BA does not only document existing workflows. A senior BA redesigns
workflows to make businesses faster, simpler, and more efficient.**

A process model explains:

\"How work happens.\"

A workflow design explains:

\"How work should move between people, systems, and decisions to achieve
the business goal.\"

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What is workflow design

-   Process vs workflow difference

-   Workflow design principles

-   Human workflow vs system workflow

-   Workflow optimization

-   Automation opportunity identification

-   Approval workflow design

-   Exception handling

-   Healthcare AI workflow example

**1. What is Workflow Design?**

Definition:

Workflow design is the process of defining the sequence of activities,
responsibilities, decisions, information movement, and system
interactions required to complete a business process.

Simple:

Workflow answers:

-   Who does the work?

-   What happens next?

-   What information is needed?

-   What decisions are required?

-   What happens when something fails?

**Example**

Business Requirement:

\"We need faster adverse event case processing.\"

A BA designs workflow:

Case Received

↓

AI Extraction

↓

Reviewer Validation

↓

Risk Assessment

↓

Medical Approval

↓

Submission

**2. Process vs Workflow**

Important BA interview topic.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Process**                        **Workflow**
  ---------------------------------- ------------------------------------
  Business activity sequence         Movement of work

  Higher-level view                  Detailed execution

  Business focused                   Operational/system focused

  \"What happens\"                   \"How it happens\"
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Process:**

Patient onboarding

**Workflow:**

Registration

↓

Identity Verification

↓

Insurance Check

↓

Doctor Assignment

↓

Appointment Confirmation

**3. Workflow Components**

A workflow contains:

1\. Trigger

2\. Tasks

3\. Actors

4\. Rules

5\. Decisions

6\. Data

7\. Outputs

8\. Exceptions

**4. Workflow Trigger**

A trigger starts the workflow.

Examples:

**User Trigger**

User submits request.

Example:

\"Upload adverse event report\"

**System Trigger**

System event.

Example:

\"New email received\"

**Time Trigger**

Scheduled event.

Example:

\"Generate weekly compliance report\"

**5. Workflow Actors**

Actors are people or systems involved.

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Actor**                         **Responsibility**
  --------------------------------- -------------------------------------
  Safety Reviewer                   Validate AI output

  AI Engine                         Extract information

  Medical Reviewer                  Approve assessment

  Regulatory System                 Receive submission
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**6. Human Workflow vs System Workflow**

Modern products combine both.

**Human Workflow**

Performed by people.

Examples:

-   Review

-   Approval

-   Decision making

-   Investigation

**System Workflow**

Performed automatically.

Examples:

-   Data extraction

-   Calculation

-   Notification

-   Report generation

**Example:**

Before AI:

Reviewer

↓

Reads document

↓

Extracts information

↓

Creates summary

After AI:

System

↓

Extracts information

↓

Generates summary

Reviewer

↓

Validates

**7. Workflow Optimization**

A BA analyzes:

**Step 1: Identify Problems**

Example:

Current workflow:

10 steps

Processing time:

5 days

**Step 2: Find Bottlenecks**

Questions:

-   Where does work wait?

-   Which step takes longest?

-   Which activity repeats?

Example:

Medical approval takes 3 days.

**Step 3: Improve Workflow**

Options:

-   Automation

-   Parallel processing

-   Removing unnecessary approvals

-   Better notifications

**8. Workflow Bottleneck Analysis**

Example:

Current:

Receive Case

↓

Data Entry (1 day)

↓

Review (1 day)

↓

Approval Queue (3 days)

↓

Submit

Problem:

Approval queue delay.

Improvement:

Add automated prioritization.

**9. Automation Opportunity Identification**

A BA should identify:

\"What can be automated?\"

Good candidates:

**Repetitive Tasks**

Example:

Data extraction

**Rule-Based Tasks**

Example:

Risk classification

**High Volume Tasks**

Example:

Thousands of reports

**Error-Prone Tasks**

Example:

Manual copying

**Poor Automation Candidates:**

Tasks requiring:

-   Human judgment

-   Ethical decisions

-   Complex reasoning

Example:

Final medical approval.

Keep human.

**10. Human-in-the-Loop Workflow**

Very important for AI products.

AI does not replace humans completely.

The workflow:

AI Prediction

↓

Confidence Check

↓

High Confidence

↓

Automatic Assistance

Low Confidence

↓

Human Review

Example:

AI risk score:

95% confidence:

Assist reviewer

AI risk score:

55% confidence:

Mandatory human review

**11. Approval Workflow Design**

Many enterprise systems use approval workflows.

Example:

Drug safety report approval.

Workflow:

Draft Report

↓

Safety Reviewer Approval

↓

Medical Reviewer Approval

↓

Regulatory Approval

↓

Submit

**Approval Rules**

BA defines:

-   Who approves?

-   When approval required?

-   What happens if rejected?

-   What happens if delayed?

**12. Exception Handling**

A professional workflow must handle failures.

Question:

\"What happens when something goes wrong?\"

Example:

AI processing failure.

Normal workflow:

Upload Document

↓

AI Processing

↓

Review

Exception:

AI Processing Failed

↓

Notify User

↓

Manual Processing

↓

Continue Workflow

**13. Parallel Workflow Design**

Sometimes activities happen simultaneously.

Example:

After case submission:

Case Received

\|

Parallel Gateway

/ \\

AI Analysis Compliance Check

\\ /

\|

Final Review

Benefits:

-   Faster processing

-   Reduced waiting time

**14. Workflow Rules**

Rules define system behavior.

Example:

Business Rule:

\"If case severity is serious, medical review is mandatory.\"

Workflow:

Risk Assessment

↓

Severity?

↓

Serious → Medical Review

Normal → Continue

**15. Workflow Documentation**

A BA documents:

**Workflow Diagram**

Visual flow

**Business Rules**

Decision logic

**User Stories**

Development requirements

**Acceptance Criteria**

Validation conditions

**SOP Updates**

Operational instructions

**16. Complete Healthcare AI Workflow Example**

**Project:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

**Current Workflow**

Receive Report

↓

Manual Data Entry

↓

Manual Classification

↓

Reviewer Analysis

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submission

Problems:

-   Slow

-   Manual errors

-   Repeated effort

**Future Workflow**

Receive Report

↓

AI Extracts Data

↓

AI Generates Summary

↓

AI Calculates Risk

↓

Confidence Check

↓

High Confidence

↓

Reviewer Validation

Low Confidence

↓

Manual Review

↓

Medical Approval

↓

Regulatory Submission

**17. BA Role in Workflow Design**

A BA:

✅ Maps current workflow\
✅ Finds inefficiencies\
✅ Defines future workflow\
✅ Identifies automation\
✅ Defines business rules\
✅ Coordinates with UX and development teams

**Interview Questions**

**Q1. Difference between process and workflow?**

Answer:

\"Process describes business activities at a high level, while workflow
defines the detailed movement of tasks, information, and
responsibilities.\"

**Q2. How do you identify automation opportunities?**

Answer:

\"I analyze repetitive, rule-based, high-volume, and error-prone
activities while considering where human judgment is required.\"

**Q3. Why is exception handling important?**

Answer:

\"Exception handling ensures the workflow continues correctly when
unexpected situations, failures, or special cases occur.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Design workflow for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Case Processing**

Create:

**Current Workflow**

Include:

-   Manual steps

-   Delays

-   Bottlenecks

**Future Workflow**

Include:

-   AI automation

-   Human validation

-   Approval steps

-   Exception handling

**Define:**

5 Business Rules

Example:

\"If AI confidence score is below 70%, manual review is required.\"

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ Workflow design\
✅ Process vs workflow\
✅ Workflow components\
✅ Human vs system workflow\
✅ Automation analysis\
✅ Approval workflows\
✅ Exception handling\
✅ Parallel workflows\
✅ Business rules\
✅ AI workflow design

**Lesson 34: UML for Business Analysts Masterclass**

**Modeling System Behavior, User Interaction & Requirements**

**BPMN explains how the business process works. UML explains how users
and systems interact to deliver that process.**

A senior BA uses UML to bridge the gap between:

**Business Need → System Behavior → Development Understanding**

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What is UML

-   Why BAs use UML

-   UML vs BPMN

-   Important UML diagrams for BAs

-   Use Case Diagram

-   Activity Diagram

-   Sequence Diagram

-   Class Diagram basics

-   State Diagram basics

-   Healthcare AI examples

**1. What is UML?**

UML stands for:

**Unified Modeling Language**

Definition:

UML is a standardized visual modeling language used to describe the
structure, behavior, and interaction of software systems.

Simple:

UML helps answer:

-   Who uses the system?

-   What can users do?

-   How do systems communicate?

-   What data objects exist?

-   How does a system change state?

**2. Why Does a BA Need UML?**

A BA uses UML to:

✅ Clarify requirements\
✅ Visualize system behavior\
✅ Reduce ambiguity\
✅ Communicate with developers\
✅ Support solution design\
✅ Improve documentation quality

Example:

Requirement:

\"System should allow AI case review.\"

This is incomplete.

UML helps define:

-   Who reviews?

-   What steps occur?

-   What systems interact?

-   What happens after approval?

**3. BPMN vs UML**

Important interview question.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **BPMN**                        **UML**
  ------------------------------- ---------------------------------------
  Business process modeling       Software/system modeling

  Focuses on workflow             Focuses on system behavior

  Used by business teams          Used by BA + technical teams

  Shows activities                Shows interactions and structure
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**BPMN:**

\"How a safety report moves through the organization\"

**UML:**

\"How reviewer, application, and AI engine communicate\"

**4. UML Diagram Types**

UML has many diagrams, but BAs mainly use:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Diagram**                     **Purpose**
  ------------------------------- ---------------------------------------
  Use Case Diagram                User interactions

  Activity Diagram                Workflow logic

  Sequence Diagram                System communication

  Class Diagram                   Data structure

  State Diagram                   Object lifecycle
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**PART 1: Use Case Diagram**

**5. What is a Use Case Diagram?**

Definition:

A Use Case Diagram shows how users (actors) interact with a system and
what functions the system provides.

It answers:

\"Who uses the system and what do they do?\"

**6. Use Case Elements**

Three main elements:

**Actor**

A person or external system.

Example:

-   Safety Reviewer

-   Medical Reviewer

-   Regulatory System

**System Boundary**

The application being built.

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Use Case**

Function performed by the system.

Example:

-   Upload Report

-   Generate AI Summary

-   Review Risk Score

**7. Healthcare AI Use Case Diagram Example**

System:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

Actors:

Safety Reviewer

Medical Reviewer

Administrator

Regulatory System

Use Cases:

Upload Case

Generate AI Summary

Review AI Recommendation

Approve Case

Manage Users

Submit Report

Visual:

Safety Reviewer

\|

\|

Upload Case

\|

\|

AI Pharmacovigilance System

\|

\|

Generate Summary

\|

\|

Review Risk

Medical Reviewer

\|

\|

Approve Case

**8. Include and Extend Relationships**

Advanced BA concept.

**Include**

Mandatory reusable behavior.

Meaning:

\"This always happens.\"

Example:

Login

included in:

-   Upload case

-   Review case

Diagram:

Review Case

\|

\|

\<\<include\>\>

\|

Login

**Extend**

Optional behavior.

Meaning:

\"This happens only sometimes.\"

Example:

Normal case review

extends:

Additional medical review

**PART 2: Activity Diagram**

**9. What is Activity Diagram?**

An activity diagram represents:

-   Actions

-   Decisions

-   Parallel flows

It looks similar to a workflow.

Example:

AI Case Processing:

Start

↓

Upload Document

↓

Validate Document

↓

Decision:

Valid?

/ \\

Yes No

\| \|

Process Error

\|

Generate Summary

\|

End

**10. Activity Diagram vs BPMN**

They look similar.

Difference:

BPMN:

Business process perspective

Activity Diagram:

System behavior perspective

Example:

BPMN:

\"Reviewer approves case\"

Activity Diagram:

System actions during approval.

**PART 3: Sequence Diagram**

**11. What is Sequence Diagram?**

Definition:

A sequence diagram shows how different actors and systems communicate
over time.

It answers:

\"What happens first, second, third?\"

**12. Sequence Diagram Example**

Scenario:

Generate AI Summary

Participants:

-   Reviewer

-   Application

-   AI Engine

Flow:

Reviewer

\|

\| Upload Document

↓

Application

\|

\| Send Document

↓

AI Engine

\|

\| Generate Summary

↓

Application

\|

\| Display Summary

↓

Reviewer

**13. Sequence Diagram Importance for BA**

Useful for:

-   API requirements

-   System integration

-   Error handling

-   Technical discussions

Example:

External Drug Database Integration:

Application

\|

\| Request Drug Information

↓

Drug Database

\|

\| Return Data

↓

Application

**PART 4: Class Diagram Basics**

**14. What is Class Diagram?**

A class diagram represents:

-   Data objects

-   Attributes

-   Relationships

Example:

Healthcare System:

Class:

Patient

Attributes:

Patient_ID

Name

Age

History

Class:

Drug

Attributes:

Drug_ID

Name

Manufacturer

Relationship:

Patient

\|

Reports

\|

Adverse Event

\|

Related To

\|

Drug

**15. Why BA Needs Class Diagrams?**

Useful for:

-   Data requirements

-   Database discussions

-   Understanding entities

-   Integration planning

**PART 5: State Diagram**

**16. What is State Diagram?**

Shows how an object changes state during its lifecycle.

Example:

Adverse Event Case:

New

↓

Under Review

↓

Validated

↓

Approved

↓

Submitted

Possible exceptions:

Rejected

Returned for Correction

**17. Complete UML Example:**

**AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Use Case Diagram**

Actors:

-   Safety Reviewer

-   Medical Reviewer

-   Admin

Functions:

-   Upload case

-   Review AI summary

-   Approve case

-   Generate report

**Activity Diagram**

Shows:

Case processing flow.

**Sequence Diagram**

Shows:

Reviewer ↔ Application ↔ AI Engine communication.

**Class Diagram**

Shows:

Patient, Drug, Case, Report entities.

**State Diagram**

Shows:

Case lifecycle.

**18. When Should a BA Use UML?**

Use UML when:

✅ System behavior needs clarification\
✅ Multiple systems interact\
✅ Developers need technical understanding\
✅ Data relationships are complex\
✅ Requirements are ambiguous

**19. Interview Questions**

**Q1. Why use UML as a BA?**

Answer:

\"UML helps BAs visually represent system behavior, user interactions,
and data relationships, improving communication between business and
technical teams.\"

**Q2. Difference between Use Case and User Story?**

Answer:

\"Use case describes system interaction in detail, while a user story
describes a user\'s need from a business perspective.\"

**Q3. Difference between Activity Diagram and Sequence Diagram?**

Answer:

\"Activity diagrams show workflow logic, while sequence diagrams show
communication between objects over time.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create:

**1. Use Case Diagram**

Actors:

-   Safety Reviewer

-   Medical Reviewer

-   Admin

Minimum:

8 use cases

**2. Sequence Diagram**

Scenario:

\"AI generates risk prediction\"

Participants:

-   User

-   Application

-   AI Engine

**3. State Diagram**

Case lifecycle:

New → Review → Approved → Submitted

Include rejection path.

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ UML fundamentals\
✅ UML vs BPMN\
✅ Use Case Diagram\
✅ Activity Diagram\
✅ Sequence Diagram\
✅ Class Diagram\
✅ State Diagram\
✅ Healthcare AI UML examples

**Lesson 35: User Journey Mapping Masterclass**

**Understanding User Goals, Pain Points & Experience Design**

**A process map shows what happens. A user journey map shows what the
user experiences while it happens.**

A senior BA does not only ask:

\"What steps does the user perform?\"

A senior BA asks:

-   What is the user trying to achieve?

-   What problems do they face?

-   What emotions do they experience?

-   Where are opportunities to improve?

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What is User Journey Mapping

-   Why BAs use journey maps

-   User Journey vs Process Flow

-   Persona creation

-   Journey stages

-   Touchpoints

-   User goals

-   Pain points

-   Emotions

-   Opportunities

-   Healthcare AI journey example

**1. What is User Journey Mapping?**

Definition:

User Journey Mapping is a visual representation of the steps a user
takes while interacting with a product or service, including their
goals, actions, thoughts, emotions, and pain points.

Simple:

It tells the story of the user\'s experience.

Example:

A doctor using an AI healthcare platform:

Need Information

↓

Login

↓

Search Patient Data

↓

Review AI Recommendation

↓

Make Decision

But journey mapping adds:

-   Frustration

-   Expectations

-   Problems

-   Opportunities

**2. Why Does a BA Need User Journey Mapping?**

A BA uses it to:

✅ Understand user problems\
✅ Discover hidden requirements\
✅ Improve user experience\
✅ Identify process gaps\
✅ Prioritize features\
✅ Collaborate with UX teams

**3. User Journey vs Process Flow**

Important distinction.

**Process Flow**

Focus:

\"What happens?\"

Example:

Upload Case

↓

AI Processing

↓

Review

↓

Approval

**User Journey**

Focus:

\"How does the user experience it?\"

Example:

Reviewer receives case

↓

Feels overwhelmed

↓

Uses AI summary

↓

Feels confident

↓

Approves case

**4. User Journey Map Components**

A professional journey map contains:

1\. Persona

2\. User Goal

3\. Journey Stages

4\. User Actions

5\. Touchpoints

6\. Thoughts

7\. Emotions

8\. Pain Points

9\. Opportunities

**PART 1: Persona Creation**

**5. What is a Persona?**

Definition:

A persona is a fictional representation of a target user based on
research and real user characteristics.

Example:

**Persona:**

**Name:**

Dr. Priya Sharma

**Role:**

Pharmacovigilance Reviewer

**Goal:**

Review safety cases accurately and quickly.

**Challenges:**

-   Too many reports

-   Manual data extraction

-   Multiple systems

**6. Why Personas Matter for BA?**

Without persona:

Requirement:

\"Build dashboard.\"

With persona:

\"For safety reviewers handling 100+ cases daily, create a dashboard
that prioritizes high-risk cases.\"

Better requirement.

**PART 2: Journey Stages**

**7. Journey Stages**

A journey is divided into phases.

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Reviewer Journey:

1\. Receive Case

2\. Review Information

3\. Analyze Risk

4\. Make Decision

5\. Submit Report

**Stage 1: Receive Case**

User Goal:

Understand assigned case.

Actions:

-   Open notification

-   Access case details

Pain Points:

-   Too many alerts

-   Missing information

Opportunity:

Smart prioritization.

**Stage 2: Review Information**

Goal:

Understand patient and drug information.

Actions:

-   Read reports

-   Search history

Pain Points:

-   Long documents

-   Manual extraction

Opportunity:

AI summary.

**Stage 3: Analyze Risk**

Goal:

Determine seriousness.

Actions:

-   Review evidence

-   Check medical information

Pain Points:

-   Complex cases

-   Lack of decision support

Opportunity:

AI risk scoring.

**Stage 4: Make Decision**

Goal:

Approve or request further review.

Actions:

-   Validate findings

-   Add comments

Pain Points:

-   Unclear reasoning

Opportunity:

Explainable AI.

**Stage 5: Submit Report**

Goal:

Complete regulatory submission.

Pain Points:

-   Manual documentation

Opportunity:

Automated reporting.

**8. Touchpoints**

A touchpoint is any interaction between user and product/service.

Examples:

AI Platform:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Touchpoint**          **Example**
  ----------------------- -----------------------------------------------
  Login                   User authentication

  Dashboard               View cases

  AI Summary              Review recommendation

  Notification            Receive alerts

  Report                  Submit case
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**9. User Thoughts**

Capture what user thinks.

Example:

Before AI:

\"I spend too much time reading documents.\"

After AI:

\"AI helps me focus on important information.\"

**10. User Emotions**

Journey mapping includes emotions.

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stage**                              **Emotion**
  -------------------------------------- --------------------------------
  Receiving 100 cases                    😟 Overwhelmed

  AI summary available                   🙂 Relieved

  Risk explanation                       😊 Confident
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**11. Pain Points**

Pain points are user problems.

Examples:

**Reviewer Pain Points:**

-   Too much manual work

-   Difficult information search

-   Duplicate data entry

-   Slow approvals

**12. Opportunities**

Convert pain points into improvements.

Example:

Pain:

\"Reviewers spend hours reading reports.\"

Opportunity:

\"Generate AI summaries.\"

Pain:

\"High-risk cases are missed.\"

Opportunity:

\"AI-based risk prioritization.\"

**13. User Journey Map Template**

Example:

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stage**     **Action**       **Pain**           **Emotion**   **Opportunity**
  ------------- ---------------- ------------------ ------------- -----------------
  Receive Case  Open case        Too many cases     😟            Smart priority

  Review        Read documents   Time consuming     😐            AI summary

  Analyze       Assess risk      Complex decisions  😟            AI scoring

  Approve       Final decision   Lack of confidence 😐            Explainable AI
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------

**14. User Journey → Requirements**

This is where BA adds value.

Example:

Journey Finding:

\"Reviewer cannot identify important information quickly.\"

↓

Requirement:

\"The system shall highlight critical safety information.\"

↓

User Story:

\"As a reviewer, I want AI-highlighted information so that I can
identify risks faster.\"

**15. Healthcare AI Complete Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

**Persona:**

Safety Reviewer

**Goal:**

Complete case review faster.

**Journey:**

Case Received

↓

Open Case

↓

AI Generates Summary

↓

Review Risk Score

↓

Validate Recommendation

↓

Approve Submission

**Key Requirements Generated:**

1.  AI summary generation

2.  Risk score display

3.  Evidence highlighting

4.  Approval workflow

5.  Audit history

**16. User Journey vs Customer Journey**

We will cover this deeply in next lesson, but basic difference:

**User Journey**

Focus:

Specific user interaction with product.

Example:

Reviewer using AI tool.

**Customer Journey**

Focus:

Entire relationship with company.

Example:

Pharma company:

Discovery → Purchase → Implementation → Support

**17. BA Interview Questions**

**Q1. Why create user journey maps?**

Answer:

\"User journey maps help understand user behavior, identify pain points,
discover requirements, and improve product experience.\"

**Q2. Difference between process map and user journey?**

Answer:

\"A process map focuses on activities and workflow, while a user journey
focuses on user experience, emotions, and interactions.\"

**Q3. How do you convert journey insights into requirements?**

Answer:

\"I identify user pain points and goals, translate them into business
needs, then create functional requirements and user stories.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a User Journey Map for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Reviewer**

Include:

**Persona**

Name:

Role:

Goals:

Challenges:

**Journey Stages**

Minimum 5 stages.

For each stage define:

-   User action

-   Touchpoint

-   Pain point

-   Emotion

-   Opportunity

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ User Journey Mapping\
✅ Persona creation\
✅ Journey stages\
✅ Touchpoints\
✅ User goals\
✅ Pain points\
✅ Emotions\
✅ Opportunity identification\
✅ Converting journeys into requirements

**Lesson 36: Customer Journey vs User Flow Masterclass**

**Understanding the Complete Experience From Business Perspective to
Product Interaction**

**A senior BA must understand three different views:**

1.  **Business Process View** → How the organization works

2.  **Customer Journey View** → How the customer experiences the
    organization

3.  **User Flow View** → How a user completes a task inside the product

Many BAs confuse these three concepts. Understanding the difference
separates a requirement writer from a product-focused BA.

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   Customer Journey Mapping

-   User Journey vs Customer Journey

-   User Flow Design

-   Customer lifecycle

-   Funnel analysis

-   Conversion points

-   Drop-off analysis

-   Healthcare digital product example

-   Converting journeys into requirements

**1. Customer Journey Mapping**

**Definition**

Customer Journey Mapping represents the complete relationship between a
customer and an organization across all stages of interaction.

It focuses on:

-   Customer expectations

-   Business touchpoints

-   Satisfaction

-   Loyalty

-   Long-term relationship

Simple:

Customer Journey answers:

\"What experience does the customer have with our company?\"

**Example:**

A pharmaceutical company adopting an AI Pharmacovigilance Platform.

Customer Journey:

Awareness

↓

Evaluation

↓

Purchase Decision

↓

Implementation

↓

Usage

↓

Support

↓

Renewal

**2. Customer Journey Components**

A professional customer journey map contains:

1\. Customer Persona

2\. Journey Stages

3\. Customer Goals

4\. Touchpoints

5\. Pain Points

6\. Emotions

7\. Business Opportunities

**3. Example: Healthcare AI Customer Journey**

**Customer:**

Global Pharmaceutical Company

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Stage 1: Awareness**

Customer Goal:

Find ways to improve safety operations.

Touchpoints:

-   Industry conference

-   Website

-   Sales presentation

Pain:

Manual processes are expensive.

Opportunity:

Show AI efficiency benefits.

**Stage 2: Evaluation**

Customer Goal:

Compare solutions.

Touchpoints:

-   Product demo

-   Technical discussion

-   Security review

Pain:

Concern about:

-   Data privacy

-   AI accuracy

Opportunity:

Provide:

-   Security documentation

-   AI validation reports

**Stage 3: Purchase Decision**

Customer Goal:

Select vendor.

Touchpoints:

-   Proposal

-   ROI calculation

-   Contract

Pain:

High investment risk.

Opportunity:

Business case demonstration.

**Stage 4: Implementation**

Customer Goal:

Deploy successfully.

Touchpoints:

-   Training

-   Configuration

-   Data migration

Pain:

User adoption issues.

Opportunity:

Change management.

**Stage 5: Usage**

Customer Goal:

Improve workflow.

Touchpoints:

-   AI dashboard

-   Reports

-   Support

Pain:

Users need confidence in AI.

Opportunity:

Explainable AI.

**4. User Journey vs Customer Journey**

Very important.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **User Journey**                 **Customer Journey**
  -------------------------------- --------------------------------------
  Focuses on product usage         Focuses on entire relationship

  Usually one user                 Customer organization/person

  Shorter timeframe                Long-term lifecycle

  Product interaction              Business interaction
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Customer Journey**

Pharma company buys AI platform.

**User Journey**

Safety reviewer uses AI platform daily.

**5. User Flow Design**

Now we go deeper into product interaction.

**Definition:**

User flow is the path a user follows to complete a specific task inside
a digital product.

It answers:

\"How does the user complete an action?\"

Example:

User Goal:

Generate AI summary.

User Flow:

Login

↓

Open Case Dashboard

↓

Select Case

↓

Click Generate Summary

↓

AI Processing

↓

View Summary

↓

Approve

**6. User Flow Components**

A user flow includes:

**Entry Point**

Where user starts.

Example:

Login screen.

**Actions**

What user does.

Example:

Click button.

**Screens**

Where interaction happens.

Example:

Dashboard.

**Decisions**

Choices user makes.

Example:

Approve or reject.

**Exit Point**

Task completion.

Example:

Summary approved.

**7. User Flow Example: AI Case Review**

Goal:

Reviewer completes case review.

Flow:

Login

↓

Dashboard

↓

View Pending Cases

↓

Select Case

↓

Review AI Summary

↓

Check Evidence

↓

Decision

\|

\|

Approve / Request Changes

\|

\|

Complete

**8. User Flow vs Workflow**

Another common confusion.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Workflow**                            **User Flow**
  --------------------------------------- -------------------------------
  Business/system process                 User interaction path

  Includes backend activities             Mostly user-facing

  Focus on operations                     Focus on usability
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

Workflow:

Case Received

↓

AI Processing

↓

Medical Approval

User Flow:

Open Dashboard

↓

Click Case

↓

Review AI Summary

↓

Approve

**9. Funnel Analysis**

A product BA also studies user movement.

A funnel shows:

How many users move from one stage to another.

Example:

AI Platform Adoption Funnel:

1000 Users Invited

↓

800 Login

↓

600 Upload Cases

↓

400 Use AI Summary

↓

300 Approve Cases

**10. Drop-off Analysis**

Drop-off means users leave before completing a task.

Example:

Problem:

Many reviewers stop using AI summary.

BA investigates.

Finds:

Users don\'t trust AI recommendations.

Requirement:

Add:

-   Confidence score

-   Explanation

-   Evidence links

**11. Conversion Points**

Conversion point:

The moment user completes a desired action.

Examples:

Healthcare App:

-   Patient books appointment

-   Doctor completes consultation

-   User subscribes

AI Product:

-   Reviewer accepts AI suggestion

-   Team adopts workflow

**12. Customer Journey → Business Requirements**

Example:

Customer Pain:

\"Implementation takes too long.\"

↓

Business Need:

Faster onboarding.

↓

Requirement:

\"The system shall provide guided setup.\"

**13. User Flow → Functional Requirements**

Example:

User Flow:

Click \"Generate Summary\"

↓

Requirement:

\"The system shall generate an AI summary after document upload.\"

**14. Complete Healthcare AI Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Customer Journey:**

Company perspective:

Discover AI

↓

Evaluate Vendor

↓

Purchase

↓

Implement

↓

Adopt

↓

Renew

**User Journey:**

Reviewer perspective:

Receive Case

↓

Review Information

↓

Analyze Risk

↓

Approve

**User Flow:**

Screen interaction:

Login

↓

Dashboard

↓

Select Case

↓

View AI Summary

↓

Approve

**15. How BA Uses All Three Together**

A senior BA connects:

Customer Journey

↓

Identifies Business Problems

User Journey

↓

Identifies User Problems

User Flow

↓

Defines Product Interaction

Requirements

↓

Development

**16. Interview Questions**

**Q1. Difference between customer journey and user journey?**

Answer:

\"Customer journey covers the complete relationship with an
organization, while user journey focuses on a user\'s interaction with a
product or service.\"

**Q2. Difference between user flow and workflow?**

Answer:

\"User flow describes the steps a user takes inside a product, while
workflow describes the movement of work across people and systems.\"

**Q3. Why should BAs understand user flows?**

Answer:

\"User flows help BAs define better functional requirements, improve
usability, and collaborate effectively with UX and development teams.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create:

**1. Customer Journey**

Stages:

-   Awareness

-   Evaluation

-   Purchase

-   Implementation

-   Adoption

-   Renewal

**2. User Journey**

Persona:

Safety Reviewer

Stages:

-   Receive case

-   Review

-   Analyze

-   Approve

**3. User Flow**

Task:

\"Generate AI Risk Assessment\"

Include:

-   Screens

-   Actions

-   Decisions

**Lesson 37: Wireframing for BA / Product Roles Masterclass**

**Translating Requirements into Visual Product Structure**

**A requirement explains what the system should do. A wireframe explains
how the user will interact with that functionality.**

A modern BA is not a UI designer, but a strong BA must understand
wireframes because they help connect:

**Business Requirement → User Need → Screen Design → Development**

**Learning Objectives**

By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

-   What is wireframing

-   Why BAs create/use wireframes

-   Wireframe types

-   Low fidelity vs high fidelity

-   Wireframe components

-   Screen requirement documentation

-   User interaction mapping

-   Collaboration with UX designers

-   Healthcare AI dashboard wireframe example

**1. What is a Wireframe?**

Definition:

A wireframe is a visual blueprint of a digital product interface that
shows the structure, layout, content placement, and user interactions
before final design and development.

Simple:

A wireframe is the **skeleton of an application screen**.

It does not focus on:

-   Colors

-   Fonts

-   Branding

It focuses on:

-   What appears where

-   What users can do

-   How users navigate

**Example**

Requirement:

\"Reviewer should see AI risk assessment.\"

Without wireframe:

Developer may create anything.

With wireframe:

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Case Review Dashboard

Patient Information

Drug Information

AI Risk Score

\[High Risk 85%\]

Evidence

\[Approve\] \[Request Review\]

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

**2. Why Does a BA Need Wireframing?**

A BA uses wireframes to:

✅ Clarify requirements\
✅ Reduce misunderstanding\
✅ Validate user expectations\
✅ Support stakeholder discussions\
✅ Collaborate with UX designers\
✅ Help developers understand screens

**3. Wireframe vs Prototype vs Mockup**

Important interview topic.

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Item**         **Purpose**                       **Detail Level**
  ---------------- --------------------------------- ---------------------
  Wireframe        Structure/layout                  Low

  Mockup           Visual design                     Medium/High

  Prototype        Interactive experience            High
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Wireframe**

Where button exists.

**Mockup**

What button looks like.

**Prototype**

How button behaves when clicked.

**4. Types of Wireframes**

There are three levels.

**Level 1: Low Fidelity Wireframe**

Also called:

Sketch wireframe

Purpose:

Early thinking.

Characteristics:

-   Simple boxes

-   No colors

-   Basic layout

Example:

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Logo

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Search Box

List

Button

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Used during:

-   Requirement discussions

-   Brainstorming

**Level 2: Mid Fidelity Wireframe**

More detailed.

Includes:

-   Real labels

-   Navigation

-   Data fields

-   Buttons

Example:

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Case ID: PV-10245

Status: Under Review

AI Risk Score: 85%

\[View Evidence\]

\[Approve Case\]

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

**Level 3: High Fidelity Wireframe**

Almost final design.

Includes:

-   Colors

-   Icons

-   Real content

-   Visual styling

Usually created by UX designers.

**5. Wireframe Components**

A BA should understand:

**1. Layout**

Where information appears.

Example:

Dashboard:

Header

Navigation

Main Content

Sidebar

Footer

**2. Navigation**

How users move.

Example:

Dashboard

↓

Cases

↓

Case Details

↓

Approval

**3. Input Fields**

Example:

Patient Report Form:

-   Patient ID

-   Drug Name

-   Event Description

-   Severity

**4. Actions**

Buttons:

-   Submit

-   Save

-   Approve

-   Reject

**5. Information Display**

Examples:

-   Tables

-   Cards

-   Charts

-   Notifications

**6. Wireframe Creation Process for BA**

A BA follows:

**Step 1: Understand User Goal**

Example:

Reviewer wants:

\"Quickly identify high-risk cases.\"

**Step 2: Identify Required Information**

Need:

-   Case ID

-   Patient details

-   Drug details

-   AI score

-   Evidence

**Step 3: Define User Actions**

User can:

-   Review

-   Approve

-   Reject

-   Escalate

**Step 4: Create Screen Structure**

Arrange:

Information + Actions

**Step 5: Validate With Stakeholders**

Ask:

\"Does this screen support your workflow?\"

**7. Screen Requirement Document**

A BA often documents screens.

Example:

**Screen Name:**

Case Review Dashboard

**Purpose:**

Allow safety reviewers to analyze AI-generated case assessments.

**Users:**

Safety Reviewer

**Components:**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Component**              **Purpose**
  -------------------------- --------------------------------------------
  Case Summary               Show patient/drug details

  Risk Score                 Display AI prediction

  Evidence Panel             Explain AI decision

  Action Buttons             Approve/reject
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**8. Healthcare AI Wireframe Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Screen:**

AI Case Review Dashboard

================================================

HEADER

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

================================================

CASE INFORMATION

Case ID: PV-45891

Patient:

Age:

Medical History:

================================================

AI ANALYSIS

Risk Score

████████ 85%

Risk Level:

HIGH

AI Explanation:

\- Serious adverse event detected

\- Previous similar cases found

================================================

EVIDENCE

Document 1

Document 2

================================================

ACTIONS

\[Approve\]

\[Request Medical Review\]

\[Reject\]

================================================

**9. BA Role vs UX Designer Role**

Important distinction.

**BA Defines:**

-   User needs

-   Business rules

-   Information required

-   Actions

-   Workflow

**UX Designer Defines:**

-   Visual style

-   Interaction patterns

-   Layout improvements

-   User experience details

Both collaborate.

**10. Wireframe and User Story Connection**

Example User Story:

\"As a safety reviewer, I want to see AI risk scores so that I can
prioritize critical cases.\"

Wireframe identifies:

Where:

AI Risk Score Card

appears.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given:

Case is processed

When:

Reviewer opens case

Then:

Risk score should display.

**11. Common Wireframe Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Designing before understanding requirements.

**Mistake 2:**

Adding too much information.

**Mistake 3:**

Ignoring user workflow.

**Mistake 4:**

Not considering errors.

Example:

What happens if AI fails?

**12. Wireframe in Agile Environment**

Where wireframes are used:

**Backlog Refinement**

Explain upcoming feature.

**Sprint Planning**

Help developers understand UI needs.

**UX Review**

Validate experience.

**UAT**

Compare delivered screen with requirement.

**13. Interview Questions**

**Q1. Why should a BA create wireframes?**

Answer:

\"Wireframes help translate business requirements into visual
structures, reduce ambiguity, and improve communication between
stakeholders, UX designers, and developers.\"

**Q2. Difference between wireframe and prototype?**

Answer:

\"A wireframe shows the structure of a screen, while a prototype
demonstrates interactive behavior.\"

**Q3. Is BA responsible for final UI design?**

Answer:

\"Usually no. BA defines requirements, workflows, and user needs. UX
designers own detailed visual design.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a low-fidelity wireframe for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Case Dashboard**

Include:

**Header**

**Case Information Section**

**AI Risk Score Section**

**Evidence Section**

**Action Buttons**

Also write:

1.  Screen purpose

2.  Users

3.  Functional requirements

4.  Acceptance criteria

✅ Wireframe types\
✅ BA role in UX collaboration\
✅ Screen requirement documentation\
✅ Dashboard design thinking\
✅ Wireframe → User Story connection\
✅ Healthcare AI example

**Lesson 17 -- Prototyping Techniques Masterclass**

**From Wireframe to Interactive Product Experience**

**1. What is Prototyping?**

Definition:

A prototype is an interactive model of a product that demonstrates how
users will interact with the system before actual development begins.

Simple:

**Wireframe = Structure**

**Prototype = Experience**

Example:

Wireframe:

Login Screen

Username

Password

\[Login Button\]

Prototype:

User clicks:

Enter username

↓

Enter password

↓

Click Login

↓

Dashboard Opens

The user can experience the flow.

**2. Why Does a BA Need Prototyping?**

A BA uses prototypes to:

✅ Validate requirements\
✅ Get stakeholder feedback early\
✅ Reduce development rework\
✅ Identify missing requirements\
✅ Explain user journeys clearly\
✅ Improve collaboration with UX and developers

Example:

Requirement:

\"The system should allow case approval.\"

Stakeholder sees prototype and says:

\"Before approval, we need an evidence review screen.\"

This requirement would have been missed without prototype validation.

**3. Wireframe vs Prototype**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Wireframe**                             **Prototype**
  ----------------------------------------- -----------------------------
  Static structure                          Interactive model

  Shows screen layout                       Shows user behavior

  Early requirement discussion              User testing

  No navigation                             Clickable navigation
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

Wireframe:

Case Dashboard

\|

\|

Approve Button

Prototype:

Dashboard

↓

Click Approve

↓

Confirmation Popup

↓

Case Status Changed

**4. Types of Prototypes**

There are four common types.

**Type 1: Paper Prototype**

Lowest fidelity.

Example:

Hand-drawn screens.

Used for:

-   Early ideas

-   Brainstorming

Example:

A rough sketch:

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

Search Patient

\[ \]

\[Search\]

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

**Type 2: Low Fidelity Prototype**

Simple digital prototype.

Contains:

-   Basic screens

-   Navigation

-   Simple interactions

Used for:

-   Requirement validation

Example:

AI Dashboard:

Login

↓

Case List

↓

Case Detail

↓

Approve

**Type 3: High Fidelity Prototype**

Near-real product experience.

Contains:

-   Real screens

-   Colors

-   Data

-   Interactions

Used for:

-   User testing

-   Stakeholder approval

**Type 4: Functional Prototype**

Almost working product.

Includes:

-   Real data

-   APIs

-   Limited functionality

Example:

AI model generating sample risk scores.

**5. Prototype Development Process**

A BA/Product team follows:

User Need

↓

Requirements

↓

Wireframe

↓

Prototype

↓

User Feedback

↓

Requirement Refinement

↓

Development

**6. Prototype Validation**

A prototype is not created just for presentation.

It is tested.

Questions:

**Can users complete the task?**

Example:

Can a safety reviewer approve a case easily?

**Is information available?**

Example:

Does reviewer see required evidence?

**Are decisions clear?**

Example:

Approve vs Reject buttons.

**7. Prototype Testing Methods**

**1. Stakeholder Review**

Business users review the prototype.

Example:

Pharmacovigilance manager reviews workflow.

**2. Usability Testing**

Real users try completing tasks.

Example:

\"Review this adverse event case.\"

Observe:

-   Time taken

-   Confusion points

-   Errors

**3. A/B Testing**

Compare two designs.

Example:

Version A:

Risk score on top.

Version B:

Risk score on sidebar.

Measure:

Which improves review speed?

**8. Figma Basics for BA**

A BA does not need to become a UI designer.

But should understand:

**Frames**

Represent screens.

Example:

Login Screen

Dashboard Screen

Case Screen

**Components**

Reusable UI elements.

Example:

Button:

\[Approve\]

Used everywhere.

**Prototype Links**

Connect screens.

Example:

Login Frame

↓

Dashboard Frame

↓

Case Detail Frame

**9. Healthcare AI Prototype Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**User:**

Safety Reviewer

**Goal:**

Review high-risk cases quickly.

Prototype Flow:

Login

↓

Dashboard

↓

High Risk Case List

↓

Case Details

↓

AI Summary

↓

Evidence View

↓

Risk Explanation

↓

Approve / Escalate

**10. Prototype → User Story**

Prototype helps create better user stories.

Example:

Prototype shows:

AI confidence score.

User Story:

As a safety reviewer, I want to see AI confidence scores so that I can
decide whether additional review is required.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given:

AI has processed a case

When:

Reviewer opens case details

Then:

Confidence score should be displayed.

**11. Prototype → Requirements**

Example:

Prototype Finding:

Reviewer needs evidence behind AI prediction.

Requirement:

\"The system shall display supporting evidence for AI-generated
recommendations.\"

**12. Common BA Prototype Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Creating screens without understanding user goals.

**Mistake 2:**

Making prototype too detailed too early.

**Mistake 3:**

Ignoring edge cases.

Example:

What happens when AI fails?

**Mistake 4:**

Not involving users.

**13. Interview Questions**

**Q1. Why create prototypes?**

Answer:

\"Prototypes help validate requirements, gather feedback, reduce
ambiguity, and ensure the final product meets user expectations.\"

**Q2. Difference between wireframe and prototype?**

Answer:

\"Wireframes represent the structure of a screen, while prototypes
simulate user interaction and product behavior.\"

**Q3. What is prototype validation?**

Answer:

\"Prototype validation is testing the design with stakeholders or users
to identify usability issues and refine requirements before
development.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a prototype flow for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Case Review**

Screens:

1.  Login

2.  Dashboard

3.  Case List

4.  Case Details

5.  AI Summary

6.  Risk Explanation

7.  Approval Screen

For each screen define:

-   Purpose

-   User action

-   Requirement

-   Acceptance criteria

✅ Prototype validation\
✅ User testing\

**PHASE 4 -- Product Design & UX Collaboration**

**Lesson 18 -- UX Requirements & Design Collaboration Masterclass**

**Bridging Business Requirements, User Experience & Product Design**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--17

Remaining:

**19 Lessons Left**

Current:

➡️ Lesson 18 of 36

**1. Why UX Matters for a Business Analyst**

Traditional BA thinking:

\"The system should perform this function.\"

Modern Product BA thinking:

\"The system should help users achieve their goal easily, efficiently,
and confidently.\"

Example:

Business Requirement:

\"Create an AI risk scoring feature.\"

Traditional requirement:

System shall calculate risk score.

UX-focused BA requirement:

Safety reviewers should be able to understand case risk within seconds
using an explainable AI risk score with supporting evidence.

**2. What Are UX Requirements?**

Definition:

UX requirements describe how the product should behave from the user\'s
perspective to provide an effective, efficient, and satisfying
experience.

They define:

-   Ease of use

-   User interaction

-   Navigation

-   Accessibility

-   User satisfaction

-   Error prevention

**3. Functional Requirement vs UX Requirement**

Very important BA concept.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Functional Requirement**          **UX Requirement**
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  What system does                    How user experiences it

  Feature behavior                    User interaction quality

  Business functionality              Usability
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Functional Requirement:**

\"The system shall allow users to upload safety reports.\"

**UX Requirement:**

\"Users should be able to upload a report within three steps with clear
progress indicators.\"

**4. Types of UX Requirements**

A BA should understand these categories:

**1. Usability Requirements**

Focus:

How easy is the system to use?

Examples:

-   User should complete case review within 5 minutes.

-   Frequently used actions should be accessible from dashboard.

**2. Accessibility Requirements**

Focus:

Can all users use the product?

Examples:

-   Support keyboard navigation.

-   Provide readable text contrast.

-   Support screen readers.

**3. Navigation Requirements**

Focus:

How users move through the system.

Example:

A reviewer should reach case details within two clicks.

**4. Interaction Requirements**

Focus:

How users interact.

Examples:

-   Confirmation before deleting data.

-   Show loading status during AI processing.

**5. Visual Requirements**

Focus:

Information presentation.

Examples:

-   Highlight critical risks.

-   Use consistent icons.

**6. Performance Experience Requirements**

Focus:

User perception.

Example:

\"The system shall display loading status if AI processing takes more
than 3 seconds.\"

**5. UX Principles Every BA Should Know**

**Principle 1: User-Centered Design**

Design around user goals.

Bad:

\"We added 20 features.\"

Good:

\"We reduced reviewer effort.\"

**Principle 2: Reduce Cognitive Load**

Users should not think unnecessarily.

Example:

Before:

Reviewer reads 20-page report.

After:

AI provides:

-   Summary

-   Important findings

-   Risk indicators

**Principle 3: Consistency**

Similar actions should look similar.

Example:

Approve button should appear consistently.

**Principle 4: Feedback**

System should tell users what is happening.

Bad:

Click button → nothing happens.

Good:

\"AI analysis in progress\...\"

**Principle 5: Error Prevention**

Prevent mistakes before they happen.

Example:

Before submitting:

\"Are you sure all required fields are completed?\"

**6. BA Collaboration With UX Designer**

A BA and UX designer work together.

**BA Provides:**

**Business Context**

Why feature is needed.

Example:

Reduce safety review time.

**User Requirements**

Who needs it.

Example:

Safety reviewer.

**Business Rules**

Example:

High-risk cases require medical approval.

**Acceptance Criteria**

Example:

Risk score must be visible before approval.

**UX Designer Provides:**

**User Interface Design**

-   Layout

-   Visual style

-   Interaction patterns

**User Experience Decisions**

-   Navigation

-   Screen hierarchy

-   User testing

**7. UX Design Collaboration Process**

Typical product workflow:

Business Problem

↓

BA Analysis

↓

User Research

↓

Requirements

↓

Wireframe

↓

Prototype

↓

UX Review

↓

Development

↓

User Testing

**8. Design Review Meetings**

A BA participates in design reviews.

Questions BA should ask:

**Does design support business goals?**

Example:

Can reviewers identify high-risk cases quickly?

**Are all requirements covered?**

Example:

Where is audit history shown?

**Are business rules reflected?**

Example:

Does high-risk approval flow exist?

**Are edge cases handled?**

Example:

What happens when AI confidence is low?

**9. UX Acceptance Criteria**

Traditional:

System shall display risk score.

Better:

Given a processed case, when the reviewer opens case details, then the
AI risk score should be displayed prominently with confidence level and
explanation.

UX criteria examples:

**Navigation**

Given user is on dashboard

When user selects a case

Then case details should open within one action.

**Error Handling**

Given AI processing fails

When user opens the case

Then system should show failure reason and manual review option.

**Feedback**

Given user uploads a report

When processing starts

Then system should display processing status.

**10. Healthcare AI Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**UX Problem:**

Reviewers struggle to understand AI recommendations.

**BA Analysis:**

User Pain:

\"I don\'t trust AI decisions.\"

**UX Requirement:**

System should provide:

-   Risk score

-   Confidence level

-   Supporting evidence

-   Explanation

**Screen Design:**

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

AI Risk Assessment

Risk Level:

HIGH

Confidence:

92%

Reason:

✓ Serious adverse event detected

✓ Similar historical cases found

Evidence:

Document A

Document B

\[Approve\]

\[Request Review\]

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

**11. UX Requirements Document Example**

A BA may document:

**Feature:**

AI Risk Dashboard

**User:**

Safety Reviewer

**Goal:**

Quickly identify serious cases.

**UX Requirements:**

UR-001:

Dashboard should highlight high-risk cases.

UR-002:

User should understand AI reasoning.

UR-003:

Critical actions require confirmation.

UR-004:

System should show processing status.

**12. Common BA Mistakes in UX Collaboration**

**Mistake 1:**

Only focusing on functionality.

**Mistake 2:**

Ignoring user emotions.

**Mistake 3:**

Giving solutions instead of understanding problems.

Example:

Wrong:

\"Add another button.\"

Correct:

\"Why does the user need this action?\"

**Mistake 4:**

Not validating with users.

**13. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is the role of BA in UX design?**

Answer:

\"A BA connects business objectives, user needs, and product
requirements while collaborating with UX designers to create usable
solutions.\"

**Q2. Difference between functional and UX requirements?**

Answer:

\"Functional requirements define what the system does, while UX
requirements define how effectively and easily users interact with the
system.\"

**Q3. How do you ensure design meets requirements?**

Answer:

\"Through design reviews, prototypes, acceptance criteria validation,
and user feedback sessions.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create UX Requirements for:

**Feature:**

AI Risk Assessment Dashboard

Define:

1.  User goal

2.  User problem

3.  Functional requirements

4.  UX requirements

5.  Acceptance criteria

Example:

Functional:

\"System shall display risk score.\"

UX:

\"Risk score should be visually prominent and understandable.\"

✅ Functional vs UX requirements\
✅ Usability requirements\
✅ Accessibility requirements\
✅ Navigation requirements\
✅ UX collaboration process\
✅ UX acceptance criteria\
✅ Healthcare AI UX example
