**Lesson 19 -- Design Thinking for Business Analysts Masterclass**

**Human-Centered Problem Solving for BA & Product Roles**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--18

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 19 of 36**

Remaining:

**17 Lessons Left**

**1. What is Design Thinking?**

Definition:

Design Thinking is a human-centered approach to solving problems by
understanding users, defining the real problem, generating ideas,
creating solutions, and testing them.

Simple:

Traditional approach:

Business decides solution

↓

Build product

↓

Users adapt

Design Thinking approach:

Understand users

↓

Find real problem

↓

Create ideas

↓

Prototype

↓

Test with users

↓

Build solution

**2. Why Does a BA Need Design Thinking?**

A traditional BA asks:

\"What requirements should we collect?\"

A Design Thinking BA asks:

\"What problem are we solving and why does the user need it?\"

Benefits for BA:

✅ Better requirement discovery\
✅ Reduces wrong assumptions\
✅ Improves user experience\
✅ Finds hidden problems\
✅ Creates innovative solutions

**3. Design Thinking Framework**

The most common model has 5 stages:

1\. Empathize

↓

2\. Define

↓

3\. Ideate

↓

4\. Prototype

↓

5\. Test

**PHASE 1: EMPATHIZE**

**Understand the User**

Goal:

Discover:

-   User problems

-   User needs

-   User behavior

-   User emotions

BA techniques used:

-   Interviews

-   Observation

-   Surveys

-   Workshops

-   User journey mapping

**Example: Pharmacovigilance AI Platform**

Business says:

\"We need AI automation.\"

BA does not immediately create requirements.

BA asks:

Who uses it?

Answer:

Safety reviewers.

Interview findings:

Reviewer says:

\"I spend 4 hours reading case documents.\"

\"I struggle to identify serious risks.\"

\"I don\'t trust automated decisions.\"

Now we understand the real problem.

**Empathy Map**

A useful BA tool.

It captures:

THINKS

\|

SAYS \-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-- DOES

\|

FEELS

Example:

**Safety Reviewer**

**Says:**

\"I have too many cases.\"

**Thinks:**

\"Will AI miss important information?\"

**Does:**

Reads documents manually.

**Feels:**

Overwhelmed and cautious.

**PHASE 2: DEFINE**

**Identify the Real Problem**

The BA converts observations into a problem statement.

Bad Problem Statement:

\"We need an AI dashboard.\"

Why?

Because it already assumes a solution.

Good Problem Statement:

\"Safety reviewers need a faster way to identify high-risk cases because
manual review takes significant time and increases workload.\"

**Problem Statement Template**

\[User\]

needs

\[Need\]

because

\[Reason/Pain\]

Example:

Safety reviewers need quick risk identification because reviewing large
volumes of reports manually is time-consuming.

**PHASE 3: IDEATE**

**Generate Solutions**

At this stage:

Do not immediately choose one solution.

Generate multiple ideas.

Techniques:

**1. Brainstorming**

Team generates ideas.

**2. Mind Mapping**

Connect related ideas.

**3. Crazy 8**

Create 8 ideas quickly.

**4. \"How Might We\" Questions**

Very important.

Example:

Problem:

Reviewers spend too much time reading reports.

How Might We:

\"How might we reduce the time needed to understand safety cases?\"

Possible ideas:

1.  AI summary

2.  Risk highlighting

3.  Automatic classification

4.  Smart search

**PHASE 4: PROTOTYPE**

**Create Solution Representation**

Prototype helps test ideas.

Examples:

-   Wireframes

-   Clickable prototypes

-   Mockups

Example:

AI Review Dashboard prototype:

Case Summary

↓

AI Risk Score

↓

Evidence Explanation

↓

Approval Decision

**PHASE 5: TEST**

**Validate With Users**

Goal:

Learn:

-   Does it solve the problem?

-   Is it easy to use?

-   What needs improvement?

Example:

Reviewer tests prototype.

Feedback:

\"Risk score is useful, but I need evidence.\"

BA updates requirements:

Add evidence explanation.

**4. Design Thinking vs Traditional BA Approach**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Traditional BA**                    **Design Thinking BA**
  ------------------------------------- ---------------------------------
  Requirement focused                   Problem focused

  Collects stated needs                 Finds hidden needs

  Starts with solution                  Starts with user

  Documents requirements                Validates assumptions
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**5. Design Thinking Tools for BA**

**1. Empathy Map**

Understand users.

**2. Customer Journey Map**

Understand experience.

**3. Persona**

Represent users.

**4. Problem Statement**

Define the challenge.

**5. User Story**

Convert needs into requirements.

**6. Prototype**

Validate solution.

**6. Healthcare AI Case Study**

**Problem:**

Pharmacovigilance team receives 50,000 reports yearly.

**Empathize Findings:**

Users:

-   Spend hours reading reports

-   Miss important signals

-   Need confidence before decisions

**Define:**

\"Safety reviewers need AI assistance to prioritize and analyze cases
faster.\"

**Ideate:**

Solutions:

-   AI extraction

-   Risk scoring

-   Similar case detection

-   Automated summaries

**Prototype:**

AI dashboard.

**Test:**

Reviewers validate workflow.

**Final Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot.

**7. Design Thinking Workshop Example**

A BA may conduct:

**Workshop Duration:**

2 hours

Participants:

-   Product Manager

-   Users

-   UX Designer

-   Developers

-   Compliance Team

Agenda:

**Step 1:**

Understand users

30 min

**Step 2:**

Define problems

30 min

**Step 3:**

Generate solutions

30 min

**Step 4:**

Select prototype idea

30 min

**8. Converting Design Thinking Output Into BA Documents**

Important connection:

Design Thinking

↓

Problem Statement

↓

Business Requirement

↓

BRD

↓

Functional Requirement

↓

User Stories

↓

Acceptance Criteria

↓

Development

**9. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is Design Thinking?**

Answer:

\"Design Thinking is a human-centered problem-solving approach that
focuses on understanding users, defining problems, creating solutions,
prototyping, and testing.\"

**Q2. Why should a BA use Design Thinking?**

Answer:

\"It helps BAs discover the real business problem, understand user
needs, and create solutions that provide better value.\"

**Q3. Difference between brainstorming and ideation?**

Answer:

\"Brainstorming is one technique used during ideation. Ideation is the
broader process of generating and exploring possible solutions.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create a Design Thinking Canvas:

**1. Empathize**

Persona:

Safety Reviewer

Find:

-   Goals

-   Pain points

-   Frustrations

**2. Define**

Create problem statement.

**3. Ideate**

Generate 5 possible solutions.

**4. Prototype**

Choose one feature.

**5. Test**

Define 5 user feedback questions.

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ Design Thinking framework\
✅ Empathy mapping\
✅ Problem framing\
✅ Ideation techniques\
✅ Prototype validation\
✅ User-centered BA approach\
✅ Healthcare AI application

**PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass**

**Lesson 20 -- Requirement Elicitation Techniques Masterclass**

**Discovering the Real Business Needs Before Writing Requirements**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--19

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 20 of 36**

Remaining:

**16 Lessons Left**

**1. What is Requirement Elicitation?**

Definition:

Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering, collecting,
understanding, and documenting the needs, expectations, and constraints
of stakeholders.

Simple:

Requirement elicitation means:

**Finding out what people actually need before building the solution.**

Example:

Stakeholder says:

\"We need an AI dashboard.\"

A junior BA writes:

\"Create dashboard.\"

A senior BA asks:

-   Who will use it?

-   What problem does it solve?

-   What decisions will they make?

-   What information do they need?

-   What success looks like?

**2. Why is Requirement Elicitation Important?**

Poor requirements cause:

❌ Wrong product\
❌ Rework\
❌ Increased cost\
❌ Delayed delivery\
❌ User dissatisfaction

Good elicitation creates:

✅ Clear scope\
✅ Better user stories\
✅ Better BRD/FRD\
✅ Better acceptance criteria\
✅ Higher product success

**3. Requirement Elicitation vs Requirement Gathering**

Important BA concept.

Many people use these terms interchangeably.

**Requirement Gathering**

Means:

Collecting already known information.

Example:

Stakeholder provides existing process documents.

**Requirement Elicitation**

Means:

Discovering hidden needs.

Example:

Through interviews, BA discovers:

\"Reviewers struggle because they cannot see historical cases.\"

A senior BA focuses more on **elicitation**.

**4. Requirement Elicitation Process**

A professional BA follows:

1\. Identify Stakeholders

↓

2\. Understand Business Problem

↓

3\. Select Elicitation Technique

↓

4\. Conduct Sessions

↓

5\. Analyze Information

↓

6\. Document Requirements

↓

7\. Validate Requirements

**5. Requirement Elicitation Techniques**

The BABOK framework identifies many techniques.

The most important for industry BAs are:

1.  Interviews

2.  Workshops

3.  Observation

4.  Surveys

5.  Document Analysis

6.  Brainstorming

7.  Focus Groups

8.  Prototyping

9.  Interface Analysis

10. Reverse Engineering

**Technique 1: Stakeholder Interviews**

**What is it?**

One-to-one discussion with stakeholders to understand needs.

**When to use?**

Best for:

-   Complex requirements

-   Expert knowledge

-   Sensitive topics

Example:

Interview:

Pharmacovigilance Manager

Questions:

-   How are cases currently reviewed?

-   What are major delays?

-   What decisions require expert judgment?

-   What reports are required?

**BA Interview Questions Framework**

**Current State**

\"How do you perform this activity today?\"

**Problems**

\"What challenges do you face?\"

**Future State**

\"What would improve this process?\"

**Success**

\"How will we measure improvement?\"

**Technique 2: Workshops**

**What is it?**

A group session where multiple stakeholders collaborate.

Participants:

-   Business users

-   Product manager

-   Developers

-   Compliance

-   UX designer

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Workshop:

Goal:

Define AI case review workflow.

Discussion:

Current:

Manual Review

↓

Medical Assessment

↓

Approval

Future:

AI Analysis

↓

Reviewer Validation

↓

Approval

**Workshop Benefits**

✅ Faster requirement discovery\
✅ Resolves conflicts\
✅ Creates shared understanding

**Technique 3: Observation**

**What is it?**

Watching users perform real tasks.

Why important?

Users may not explain everything.

Example:

BA observes safety reviewer.

Finds:

Reviewer uses:

-   Excel sheets

-   Email

-   Multiple systems

Problem discovered:

Too much manual switching.

Requirement:

\"System should provide centralized case information.\"

**Technique 4: Surveys**

Used when many users need feedback.

Example:

Healthcare app survey:

Questions:

-   How often do you review cases?

-   Biggest challenges?

-   Which features are most useful?

Advantages:

-   Large audience

-   Quick data collection

Disadvantages:

-   Less detailed answers

**Technique 5: Document Analysis**

BA reviews existing documents.

Examples:

-   SOPs

-   Policies

-   Regulations

-   Reports

-   Existing system manuals

Healthcare example:

Review:

Pharmacovigilance SOP

Find:

-   Reporting timelines

-   Approval rules

-   Compliance requirements

**Technique 6: Brainstorming**

Used for generating ideas.

Example:

Problem:

Reduce manual review time.

Ideas:

-   AI summary

-   Automation

-   Smart search

-   Classification

**Technique 7: Prototyping**

Prototype can also be an elicitation technique.

Why?

Users react better to visuals.

Example:

Show dashboard prototype.

User says:

\"We also need confidence score.\"

New requirement discovered.

**Technique 8: Interface Analysis**

Used when systems integrate.

Example:

AI platform connects with:

-   Drug database

-   EHR system

-   Regulatory portal

BA studies:

-   Data exchange

-   APIs

-   System limitations

**Technique 9: Reverse Engineering**

Used when documentation is missing.

Example:

Old legacy system.

BA studies:

-   Existing screens

-   Database

-   Reports

to understand requirements.

**6. Choosing the Right Technique**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Situation**                             **Best Technique**
  ----------------------------------------- -----------------------------
  Need expert knowledge                     Interview

  Multiple stakeholder conflict             Workshop

  Unknown user problems                     Observation

  Large user group                          Survey

  Existing process exists                   Document Analysis

  Need user feedback                        Prototype

  Old system replacement                    Reverse Engineering
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**7. Healthcare AI Case Study**

**Problem:**

Pharma company wants AI safety platform.

**Step 1: Stakeholder Identification**

Stakeholders:

-   Safety reviewers

-   Medical reviewers

-   Regulatory team

-   IT team

-   Compliance team

**Step 2: Elicitation Plan**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stakeholder**              **Technique**
  ---------------------------- ------------------------------------------
  Safety Reviewer              Interview + Observation

  Medical Team                 Workshop

  Compliance                   Document Analysis

  End Users                    Prototype Testing
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 3: Findings**

Business Need:

Reduce review time.

User Pain:

Manual document analysis.

Requirement:

AI should extract important safety information.

**8. Requirement Validation**

After elicitation:

Never directly document and send.

Validate.

Validation Questions:

-   Is requirement clear?

-   Is it complete?

-   Is it feasible?

-   Does it solve the problem?

-   Is priority correct?

**9. Common BA Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Accepting stakeholder request as requirement.

Example:

\"We need mobile app.\"

BA should ask:

\"Why?\"

**Mistake 2:**

Talking only to managers.

Users are equally important.

**Mistake 3:**

Not documenting assumptions.

**Mistake 4:**

Not validating requirements.

**10. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is requirement elicitation?**

Answer:

\"Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering stakeholder
needs, analyzing business problems, and identifying requirements before
solution development.\"

**Q2. Difference between interview and workshop?**

Answer:

\"Interview is usually one-to-one information gathering, while workshops
involve multiple stakeholders collaborating to define requirements.\"

**Q3. Why is observation useful?**

Answer:

\"Observation reveals actual user behavior and hidden problems that
users may not communicate during interviews.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create an Elicitation Plan:

**Stakeholders:**

1.  Safety Reviewer

2.  Medical Reviewer

3.  Compliance Manager

4.  IT Architect

For each define:

-   Elicitation technique

-   Questions to ask

-   Expected requirements

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ Requirement elicitation concept\
✅ Elicitation vs gathering\
✅ Interviews\
✅ Workshops\
✅ Observation\
✅ Surveys\
✅ Document analysis\
✅ Prototyping as elicitation\
✅ Healthcare AI requirement discovery

**PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass**

**Lesson 21 -- BRD (Business Requirement Document) Masterclass**

**How a Professional Business Analyst Creates a BRD**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--20

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 21 of 36**

Remaining:

**15 Lessons Left**

**1. What is a BRD?**

**Definition**

A Business Requirement Document (BRD) describes the business problem,
objectives, expected business outcomes, scope, and high-level
requirements of a project.

Simple:

BRD answers:

**\"Why are we building this solution?\"**

A BRD focuses on:

-   Business goals

-   Problems

-   Expected outcomes

-   Stakeholder needs

-   Scope

It does **not** focus on technical implementation.

**2. Where Does BRD Come in a Project?**

A typical flow:

Business Problem

↓

Business Analysis

↓

BRD

↓

FRD

↓

SRS

↓

User Stories

↓

Development

↓

Testing

↓

Release

**3. BRD vs FRD vs SRS**

Very important interview topic.

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Document**   **Main Question**                       **Audience**
  -------------- --------------------------------------- ---------------------
  BRD            Why do we need this?                    Business stakeholders

  FRD            What should the system do?              BA + Developers

  SRS            How should the system behave in detail? Technical teams
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

Business Problem:

\"Safety reviewers spend too much time reviewing adverse event cases.\"

**BRD:**

\"We need AI automation to reduce review time by 50%.\"

**FRD:**

\"The system shall generate AI summaries from uploaded reports.\"

**SRS:**

\"The system shall process PDF documents, extract entities, and generate
summary output within defined performance limits.\"

**4. Why is BRD Important?**

A good BRD provides:

✅ Clear business direction\
✅ Stakeholder alignment\
✅ Project scope definition\
✅ Business justification\
✅ Success measurement

Without BRD:

Different teams may have different expectations.

**5. Who Uses BRD?**

**Business Stakeholders**

Understand:

-   Why project exists

-   Expected benefits

**Product Manager**

Uses it for:

-   Product vision

-   Roadmap planning

**BA**

Uses it for:

-   Requirement analysis

**Development Team**

Uses it for:

-   Understanding business context

**QA Team**

Uses it for:

-   Understanding business objectives

**6. BRD Structure (Industry Format)**

A professional BRD usually contains:

**1. Document Information**

Contains:

-   Document name

-   Version

-   Author

-   Date

-   Approval status

Example:

Document:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform BRD

Version:

1.0

Author:

Business Analyst

**2. Executive Summary**

Explains:

-   Project overview

-   Business reason

Example:

The pharmaceutical company wants to implement an AI-powered
pharmacovigilance platform to improve adverse event processing
efficiency and reduce manual review effort.

**3. Business Problem Statement**

Very important section.

Template:

Current Situation:

Problem:

Impact:

Need:

Example:

**Current Situation:**

Safety teams manually review thousands of adverse event reports.

**Problem:**

Review process is slow and inconsistent.

**Impact:**

-   Delayed reporting

-   Increased operational cost

-   Reviewer workload

**Need:**

AI-assisted case processing platform.

**4. Business Objectives**

Objectives should be measurable.

Bad:

\"Improve efficiency.\"

Good:

\"Reduce average case review time by 40% within six months.\"

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Objective**                      **Measurement**
  ---------------------------------- ------------------------------------
  Reduce review time                 40% reduction

  Improve accuracy                   Reduce errors by 30%

  Improve compliance                 Faster reporting
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**5. Project Scope**

Defines boundaries.

**In Scope**

Included:

✅ AI case summarization\
✅ Risk prediction\
✅ Case prioritization\
✅ Reporting dashboard

**Out of Scope**

Not included:

❌ Drug discovery\
❌ Clinical trial management\
❌ Patient diagnosis

**6. Stakeholder Identification**

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stakeholder**                  **Role**
  -------------------------------- --------------------------------------
  Safety Manager                   Business owner

  Reviewer                         End user

  Compliance Team                  Regulatory validation

  IT Team                          Technical support
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**7. Current State Analysis (AS-IS)**

Describe current process.

Example:

Current workflow:

Report Received

↓

Manual Data Entry

↓

Reviewer Analysis

↓

Medical Review

↓

Submission

Problems:

-   Manual effort

-   Duplicate work

-   Delays

**8. Future State Vision (TO-BE)**

Expected future process.

Report Received

↓

AI Extraction

↓

AI Risk Analysis

↓

Reviewer Validation

↓

Submission

Benefits:

-   Faster processing

-   Better prioritization

-   Improved consistency

**9. Business Requirements**

These are high-level needs.

Format:

BR-001:

\"The business requires an AI-based solution to automatically analyze
safety reports.\"

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **ID**        **Requirement**
  ------------- ---------------------------------------------------------
  BR-001        Reduce manual review effort

  BR-002        Improve case prioritization

  BR-003        Maintain regulatory compliance
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**10. Assumptions and Constraints**

**Assumptions**

Things expected to be true.

Example:

-   Users will receive training.

-   Data will be available.

**Constraints**

Limitations.

Example:

-   Regulatory restrictions

-   Data privacy requirements

-   Budget limitations

**11. Success Criteria**

How do we know project succeeded?

Example:

Success:

-   50% faster review process

-   95% system availability

-   User satisfaction above 85%

**12. Risks**

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Risk**                     **Impact**    **Mitigation**
  ---------------------------- ------------- ----------------------------
  Low AI accuracy              High          Human validation

  Data privacy issue           High          Security controls

  User resistance              Medium        Training
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Complete BRD Example**

**Project:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Business Problem:**

Safety reviewers manually analyze large volumes of adverse event reports
causing delays.

**Business Objective:**

Create an AI-assisted platform to reduce case review time and improve
regulatory compliance.

**Scope:**

**In Scope:**

-   AI document extraction

-   Risk scoring

-   Review dashboard

-   Reporting support

**Out of Scope:**

-   Medical diagnosis

-   Drug development

**Stakeholders:**

-   Pharmacovigilance team

-   Medical reviewers

-   Compliance department

**Expected Benefits:**

-   Faster review

-   Better accuracy

-   Reduced workload

**13. BRD Writing Tips for BA**

**Tip 1:**

Always start with \"Why\".

**Tip 2:**

Avoid technical language.

Wrong:

\"Use NLP transformer model.\"

Correct:

\"System should automatically extract safety information from reports.\"

**Tip 3:**

Focus on business value.

**Tip 4:**

Make objectives measurable.

**14. Common BRD Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Writing features instead of business needs.

Wrong:

\"Add AI dashboard.\"

Correct:

\"Improve visibility of case risk information.\"

**Mistake 2:**

No scope definition.

**Mistake 3:**

No success criteria.

**Mistake 4:**

Mixing technical design into BRD.

**15. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is the purpose of BRD?**

Answer:

\"BRD defines business objectives, problems, scope, and high-level
requirements to align stakeholders before solution development.\"

**Q2. Difference between BRD and FRD?**

Answer:

\"BRD explains business needs and objectives, while FRD describes the
functional behavior required from the system.\"

**Q3. Who prepares BRD?**

Answer:

\"Usually a Business Analyst prepares BRD by collaborating with business
stakeholders, product owners, and subject matter experts.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create a BRD outline for:

**Project:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

Include:

1.  Executive Summary

2.  Business Problem

3.  Objectives

4.  Scope

5.  Stakeholders

6.  Current State

7.  Future State

8.  Business Requirements

9.  Success Criteria

10. Risks

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ BRD purpose\
✅ BRD structure\
✅ BRD vs FRD vs SRS\
✅ Business problem documentation\
✅ Scope definition\
✅ Stakeholder analysis\
✅ AS-IS and TO-BE\
✅ Business requirements\
✅ Real healthcare AI BRD example

**PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass**

**Lesson 22 -- FRD (Functional Requirement Document) Masterclass**

**Converting Business Needs into System Functional Requirements**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--21

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 22 of 36**

Remaining:

**14 Lessons Left**

**1. What is FRD?**

**Definition**

A Functional Requirement Document (FRD) describes what the system must
do, the functions it must provide, and how users interact with those
functions.

Simple:

BRD asks:

\"Why are we building this?\"

FRD asks:

\"What should the system do?\"

Example:

**BRD:**

\"Reduce pharmacovigilance case review time.\"

**FRD:**

\"The system shall automatically generate a summary of uploaded adverse
event reports.\"

**2. Position of FRD in Software Development**

The flow:

Business Need

↓

BRD

(Why)

↓

FRD

(What)

↓

SRS

(How in detail)

↓

Development

↓

Testing

**3. BRD vs FRD Difference**

Important interview question.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **BRD**                               **FRD**
  ------------------------------------- ---------------------------------
  Business perspective                  System perspective

  Explains goals                        Explains functionality

  High-level                            Detailed

  Written for business stakeholders     Used by developers & testers
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Business Requirement:**

\"Improve adverse event processing.\"

**Functional Requirements:**

FR-001:

System shall allow users to upload adverse event reports.

FR-002:

System shall extract patient and drug information.

FR-003:

System shall generate risk classification.

**4. Why is FRD Important?**

A good FRD helps:

✅ Developers understand features\
✅ QA create test cases\
✅ Stakeholders validate functionality\
✅ Reduce ambiguity\
✅ Control scope

**5. Who Uses FRD?**

**Business Analyst**

Creates and maintains FRD.

**Product Manager**

Ensures alignment with product goals.

**Developers**

Use it to build functionality.

**QA Engineers**

Convert requirements into test scenarios.

**UX Designers**

Understand user interactions.

**6. FRD Structure (Industry Template)**

A professional FRD contains:

**1. Document Information**

Example:

Document:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform FRD

Version:

1.0

Prepared By:

Business Analyst

**2. Introduction**

Contains:

-   Purpose

-   Background

-   Scope

Example:

This document defines functional requirements for an AI-based
pharmacovigilance system that supports adverse event processing and
review.

**3. System Overview**

Describe major modules.

Example:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Modules:

1.  Case Management

2.  AI Analysis Engine

3.  Review Dashboard

4.  Reporting Module

5.  User Management

**4. User Roles**

Define who interacts with system.

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Role**                         **Responsibility**
  -------------------------------- --------------------------------------
  Safety Reviewer                  Review cases

  Medical Reviewer                 Validate decisions

  Admin                            Manage users

  Compliance Officer               Regulatory monitoring
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**5. Functional Requirements**

Main section.

Format:

Requirement ID

Description

Priority

User Role

Acceptance Criteria

Example:

**FR-001**

**Requirement:**

The system shall allow safety reviewers to upload adverse event reports.

**Priority:**

High

**User:**

Safety Reviewer

**Acceptance Criteria:**

Given:

User is logged in

When:

User uploads PDF report

Then:

System stores the document successfully.

**7. Functional Requirement Categories**

A BA usually organizes requirements by module.

**Module 1: User Authentication**

**FR-001**

System shall allow users to login using authorized credentials.

**FR-002**

System shall lock account after multiple failed attempts.

**Module 2: Case Management**

**FR-003**

System shall allow users to create a new safety case.

**FR-004**

System shall assign unique case ID automatically.

**Module 3: AI Analysis**

**FR-005**

System shall extract safety information from uploaded documents.

**FR-006**

System shall generate AI risk assessment.

**Module 4: Review Dashboard**

**FR-007**

System shall display pending cases.

**FR-008**

System shall allow reviewers to approve or reject cases.

**Module 5: Reporting**

**FR-009**

System shall generate regulatory reports.

**8. Business Rules in FRD**

Business rules define decisions.

Example:

Rule:

High-risk cases require medical approval.

Document:

BR-001:

IF risk score \> 80%

THEN

Medical reviewer approval required.

Other examples:

-   Mandatory fields

-   Approval rules

-   Validation rules

-   Eligibility criteria

**9. Use Cases in FRD**

Many FRDs include use cases.

Example:

**Use Case:**

Generate AI Risk Assessment

**Actor:**

Safety Reviewer

**Pre-condition:**

Case document uploaded.

**Main Flow:**

1.  User opens case.

2.  User clicks \"Generate Assessment\".

3.  AI analyzes document.

4.  System displays risk score.

**Alternative Flow:**

AI processing fails.

System shows error message.

**10. FRD Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Feature:**

AI Case Summarization

**Functional Requirement:**

FR-AI-001

The system shall generate a summary from uploaded adverse event
documents.

**User:**

Safety Reviewer

**Input:**

PDF safety report

**Process:**

AI extracts:

-   Patient information

-   Drug information

-   Adverse event details

**Output:**

Summary displayed on dashboard.

**Acceptance Criteria:**

Given a valid document

When user selects \"Generate Summary\"

Then system displays AI-generated summary.

**11. FRD Writing Best Practices**

**1. Use \"The system shall\"**

Professional standard.

Example:

\"The system shall display case status.\"

**2. Avoid vague words**

Avoid:

-   Fast

-   Easy

-   User-friendly

Instead:

Define measurable criteria.

Bad:

\"The system shall load quickly.\"

Good:

\"The system shall display dashboard within 3 seconds.\"

**3. One requirement = One behavior**

Bad:

\"The system shall upload documents and analyze and approve cases.\"

Too many functions.

Better:

FR-001 Upload document.

FR-002 Analyze document.

FR-003 Approve case.

**12. Common FRD Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Writing business goals instead of functions.

Wrong:

\"Improve compliance.\"

Correct:

\"System shall generate regulatory submission report.\"

**Mistake 2:**

Missing acceptance criteria.

**Mistake 3:**

Not defining user roles.

**Mistake 4:**

Combining multiple requirements.

**13. BRD → FRD Conversion Example**

**BRD Statement:**

\"Reduce manual pharmacovigilance review effort.\"

↓

BA Analysis:

Need automation.

↓

FRD Requirements:

FR-001:

System shall extract data from safety reports.

FR-002:

System shall generate AI summary.

FR-003:

System shall highlight critical findings.

FR-004:

System shall prioritize high-risk cases.

**14. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is FRD?**

Answer:

\"FRD describes the functional behavior of a system, including features,
user interactions, business rules, and expected outputs.\"

**Q2. Difference between BRD and FRD?**

Answer:

\"BRD defines business objectives and needs, while FRD translates those
needs into system functionality.\"

**Q3. Who creates FRD?**

Answer:

\"Typically the Business Analyst creates FRD by collaborating with
stakeholders, product owners, UX teams, and technical teams.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Prepare an FRD section for:

**Feature:**

AI Risk Assessment Dashboard

Create:

1.  Functional Requirements (minimum 5)

2.  User Roles

3.  Business Rules

4.  Acceptance Criteria

Example:

FR-001:

System shall display AI risk score.

Acceptance:

Given case is analyzed,

When reviewer opens case,

Then risk score is displayed.

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ FRD purpose\
✅ BRD vs FRD\
✅ FRD structure\
✅ Functional requirement writing\
✅ Business rules\
✅ Use cases\
✅ Acceptance criteria connection\
✅ Healthcare AI FRD example

**PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass**

**Lesson 23 -- SRS (Software Requirement Specification) Masterclass**

**Converting Functional Requirements into Detailed System
Specifications**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--22

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 23 of 36**

Remaining:

**13 Lessons Left**

**1. What is SRS?**

**Definition**

Software Requirement Specification (SRS) is a detailed document that
describes the complete behavior, functionality, constraints, interfaces,
and quality requirements of a software system.

Simple:

BRD:

Why are we building it?

FRD:

What should the system do?

SRS:

How exactly should the software behave?

**2. Requirement Documentation Hierarchy**

Professional BA flow:

Business Problem

↓

BRD

(Business Need)

↓

FRD

(System Functions)

↓

SRS

(Detailed Specification)

↓

User Stories

(Agile Execution)

↓

Development

↓

Testing

**3. BRD vs FRD vs SRS**

Very important interview topic.

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Document**   **Main Question**                         **Detail
                                                           Level**
  -------------- ----------------------------------------- ---------------
  BRD            Why do we need this?                      High-level

  FRD            What functionality is needed?             Medium

  SRS            How should the software behave?           Detailed
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

**Healthcare AI Example**

Business Requirement:

\"Reduce manual safety review effort.\"

**BRD:**

\"The organization needs AI assistance to improve pharmacovigilance
operations.\"

**FRD:**

\"The system shall generate AI summaries for safety cases.\"

**SRS:**

\"The system shall accept PDF, DOCX, and XML files up to 20MB, extract
safety information, generate a summary within 30 seconds, and display
confidence scores.\"

**4. Why is SRS Important?**

SRS helps:

✅ Developers understand exact expectations\
✅ QA create detailed test cases\
✅ Architects design solutions\
✅ Stakeholders validate scope\
✅ Reduce requirement ambiguity

**5. Who Uses SRS?**

**Business Analyst**

Creates and manages requirements.

**Product Manager**

Ensures product alignment.

**Developers**

Use specifications for implementation.

**QA Engineers**

Create test scenarios.

**Architects**

Design technical solution.

**6. SRS Structure (Industry Template)**

A professional SRS contains:

**Section 1: Introduction**

Includes:

-   Purpose

-   Scope

-   Definitions

-   References

Example:

**Purpose**

This document defines software requirements for an AI-powered
pharmacovigilance platform.

**Section 2: Product Overview**

Describes:

-   System background

-   Product vision

-   User groups

Example:

System:

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

Purpose:

Assist safety teams in analyzing adverse event reports.

**Section 3: User Roles and Permissions**

Defines access.

Example:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Role**                       **Access**
  ------------------------------ ----------------------------------------
  Safety Reviewer                Review cases

  Medical Reviewer               Approve high-risk cases

  Admin                          Manage users
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Section 4: Functional Requirements**

Detailed system behavior.

Example:

**SRS-FR-001**

**Feature:**

Document Upload

**Requirement:**

The system shall allow safety reviewers to upload adverse event
documents.

**Input:**

PDF, DOCX files

**Validation:**

Maximum size: 20 MB

**Output:**

Document stored successfully.

**Section 5: Non-Functional Requirements**

Defines quality expectations.

Examples:

-   Performance

-   Security

-   Reliability

-   Scalability

Example:

**Performance**

\"The dashboard shall load within 3 seconds.\"

**Security**

\"User authentication shall use multi-factor authentication.\"

**Availability**

\"The system shall maintain 99.5% availability.\"

**Section 6: External Interface Requirements**

Defines system interactions.

Types:

**User Interface**

Example:

Dashboard screens.

**API Interface**

Example:

Connect with regulatory reporting system.

**Database Interface**

Example:

Store case information.

**External Systems**

Example:

Drug database integration.

**Section 7: Data Requirements**

Defines information handled by system.

Example:

**Case Entity**

Fields:

-   Case ID

-   Patient ID

-   Drug Name

-   Event Description

-   Risk Level

**Section 8: Business Rules**

Example:

Rule:

High-risk cases require medical approval.

Specification:

IF Risk Score \>= 80%

THEN

Medical Review Required

**Section 9: Assumptions and Constraints**

**Assumptions:**

-   Users have valid accounts.

-   Required data is available.

**Constraints:**

-   Regulatory compliance requirements.

-   Data privacy restrictions.

**Section 10: Acceptance Criteria**

Defines when requirement is complete.

Example:

Requirement:

AI Risk Score Display

Acceptance Criteria:

Given:

Case analysis is completed.

When:

Reviewer opens case.

Then:

Risk score and explanation are displayed.

**7. SRS Example -- AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Feature:**

AI Risk Assessment

**Functional Specification**

Requirement:

The system shall analyze safety cases using AI models.

**Input:**

-   Patient information

-   Drug information

-   Medical event details

**Processing:**

AI analyzes:

-   Severity

-   Historical patterns

-   Similar cases

**Output:**

Display:

-   Risk score

-   Risk category

-   Explanation

**Performance Requirement:**

Response should be available within 30 seconds.

**Security Requirement:**

Only authorized reviewers can access case information.

**8. SRS and Agile Projects**

In traditional projects:

SRS is created before development.

In Agile:

Large SRS may be replaced by:

-   Epics

-   User Stories

-   Acceptance Criteria

-   Backlog items

But enterprise projects still use SRS.

Examples:

-   Healthcare

-   Banking

-   Government

-   Regulatory systems

**9. SRS Writing Rules for BA**

**Rule 1:**

Be precise.

Bad:

\"The system should be fast.\"

Good:

\"The system shall respond within 3 seconds.\"

**Rule 2:**

Avoid assumptions.

Bad:

\"Users know the process.\"

Good:

\"System shall provide onboarding instructions.\"

**Rule 3:**

Use measurable statements.

**Rule 4:**

Define exceptions.

Example:

Normal:

Upload successful.

Exception:

Invalid file format.

**10. Common SRS Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Copying BRD into SRS.

**Mistake 2:**

Missing non-functional requirements.

**Mistake 3:**

Not defining integrations.

**Mistake 4:**

Ambiguous language.

**11. BRD → FRD → SRS Complete Example**

**Business Goal:**

Improve pharmacovigilance efficiency.

↓

**BRD:**

Need AI support for safety review.

↓

**FRD:**

System shall generate AI case summaries.

↓

**SRS:**

System shall:

-   Accept PDF/DOCX files

-   Extract medical entities

-   Generate summary within 30 seconds

-   Display confidence score

-   Store audit history

**12. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is SRS?**

Answer:

\"SRS is a detailed specification document that defines functional and
non-functional requirements, system behavior, interfaces, constraints,
and quality expectations.\"

**Q2. Difference between FRD and SRS?**

Answer:

\"FRD defines required system functions, while SRS provides detailed
technical and behavioral specifications of those functions.\"

**Q3. Is SRS required in Agile?**

Answer:

\"Agile teams usually use lightweight documentation like user stories
and acceptance criteria, but detailed SRS is still used in regulated
industries.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create SRS for:

**Feature:**

AI Case Risk Assessment Module

Include:

**Functional Requirements**

Minimum 5:

-   Input

-   Processing

-   Output

**Non-functional Requirements**

Minimum 5:

-   Performance

-   Security

-   Availability

-   Scalability

-   Compliance

**Business Rules**

Minimum 3.

**Lesson Summary**

Today you learned:

✅ SRS purpose\
✅ BRD vs FRD vs SRS\
✅ SRS structure\
✅ Functional specifications\
✅ Non-functional requirements introduction\
✅ Interface requirements\
✅ Data requirements\
✅ Healthcare AI SRS example

**Lesson 24 -- Advanced User Stories Masterclass**

**Writing Industry-Level Agile Requirements as a BA/Product Analyst**

**Module Progress Reminder**

**Total Lessons:** 36

✅ Lesson 1--23

Current:

**12 Lessons Left**

**1. What is a User Story?**

**Definition**

A user story is a short description of a product requirement written
from the perspective of the user, explaining who needs something, what
they need, and why they need it.

Standard format:

As a \<user role\>

I want \<goal/action\>

So that \<business value\>

Example:

As a safety reviewer,\
I want AI-generated case summaries,\
so that I can review adverse event reports faster.

**2. Why Do We Use User Stories?**

Traditional documents:

BRD → FRD → SRS

are useful for enterprise projects.

Agile teams need smaller, actionable requirements.

User stories help:

✅ Developers understand user needs\
✅ Product owners prioritize work\
✅ QA create tests\
✅ Teams deliver incremental value

**3. User Story vs Requirement**

Important BA concept.

A requirement:

The system shall generate risk scores.

A user story:

As a safety reviewer, I want to see AI risk scores so that I can
prioritize high-risk cases.

Difference:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Requirement**                         **User Story**
  --------------------------------------- -------------------------------
  System-focused                          User-focused

  Describes functionality                 Describes user value

  Traditional documentation               Agile backlog item
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**4. User Story Hierarchy**

Large products use hierarchy.

Epic

↓

Feature

↓

User Story

↓

Task

**Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**Epic:**

AI-Assisted Case Review

**Feature:**

AI Risk Assessment

**User Stories:**

Story 1:

As a reviewer, I want AI risk scores so that I can prioritize cases.

Story 2:

As a reviewer, I want risk explanations so that I can trust AI
recommendations.

Story 3:

As a medical reviewer, I want approval workflow so that high-risk cases
receive validation.

**5. Components of a Good User Story**

A professional user story contains:

**1. User Role**

Who needs it?

Example:

Safety Reviewer

**2. Goal**

What does the user want?

Example:

View AI risk score

**3. Business Value**

Why does it matter?

Example:

Prioritize important cases quickly.

**4. Acceptance Criteria**

How do we know it is complete?

Example:

Given:

Case has been analyzed

When:

Reviewer opens case

Then:

Risk score is displayed.

**6. INVEST Principle**

A good user story follows INVEST.

**I -- Independent**

Should be developed separately.

Bad:

\"Login and dashboard and reporting.\"

Too large.

Better:

Story 1:

Login functionality.

Story 2:

Dashboard access.

Story 3:

Report generation.

**N -- Negotiable**

Details can be discussed.

A story is not a fixed technical design.

**V -- Valuable**

Must provide user/business value.

Bad:

\"As a user, I want a database.\"

No user value.

Good:

\"As a reviewer, I want previous case history so that I can make better
decisions.\"

**E -- Estimable**

Team should estimate effort.

**S -- Small**

Should fit into a sprint.

**T -- Testable**

Must have clear validation.

**7. Writing Good User Stories**

Weak:

As a user, I want a dashboard.

Problem:

-   Which user?

-   Why?

-   What information?

Better:

As a safety manager, I want a case monitoring dashboard so that I can
track pending high-risk cases.

**8. User Story Examples -- Healthcare AI**

**Story 1: AI Summary**

**User Story**

As a safety reviewer,

I want AI-generated case summaries,

so that I can understand reports quickly.

**Acceptance Criteria**

Given:

A safety report is uploaded.

When:

AI processing completes.

Then:

The system displays a summary containing:

-   Patient information

-   Drug information

-   Event details

**Story 2: Risk Classification**

**User Story**

As a reviewer,

I want AI risk classification,

so that I can prioritize serious cases.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given:

A case is analyzed.

When:

Risk assessment completes.

Then:

System displays:

-   Risk category

-   Confidence score

-   Explanation

**Story 3: Audit Trail**

**User Story**

As a compliance officer,

I want activity history,

so that I can verify regulatory compliance.

Acceptance Criteria:

System records:

-   User action

-   Timestamp

-   Change history

**9. Advanced User Story Template Used in Industry**

Story ID:

Title:

Epic:

User Story:

As a\...

I want\...

So that\...

Business Value:

Priority:

Dependencies:

Assumptions:

Acceptance Criteria:

Definition of Done:

Example:

**Story ID:**

PV-AI-001

**Title:**

AI Case Summary Generation

**Epic:**

AI Case Processing

**User Story:**

As a safety reviewer,

I want AI-generated summaries,

so that I can reduce manual reading effort.

**Priority:**

High

**Acceptance Criteria:**

1.  User uploads report.

2.  AI processes document.

3.  Summary is generated.

4.  Reviewer can edit summary.

**10. User Story Splitting Techniques**

Large stories should be split.

Example:

Large Story:

\"As a reviewer, I want complete AI case management.\"

Too large.

Split:

1.  Upload case document.

2.  Extract information.

3.  Generate summary.

4.  Display risk score.

5.  Approve case.

Techniques:

**Split by Workflow**

Example:

Create → Review → Approve

**Split by User Role**

Example:

Reviewer vs Manager

**Split by Data Type**

Example:

Patient data vs Drug data

**11. User Story vs Use Case**

Another interview topic.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **User Story**                   **Use Case**
  -------------------------------- --------------------------------------
  Agile requirement                Detailed interaction

  Short                            Detailed

  Sprint planning                  System analysis

  User value focused               Actor-system behavior
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

User Story:

\"Reviewer wants risk score.\"

Use Case:

Detailed steps of how system generates and displays risk score.

**12. Common BA Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Writing technical stories.

Wrong:

\"As a developer, I want database optimization.\"

**Mistake 2:**

No business value.

**Mistake 3:**

Too large stories.

**Mistake 4:**

Missing acceptance criteria.

**13. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is a user story?**

Answer:

\"A user story is an Agile requirement describing user needs, desired
functionality, and business value.\"

**Q2. What is INVEST?**

Answer:

\"INVEST defines characteristics of good user stories: Independent,
Negotiable, Valuable, Estimable, Small, and Testable.\"

**Q3. Difference between Epic and User Story?**

Answer:

\"An Epic is a large business capability that contains multiple smaller
user stories deliverable within sprints.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create User Stories for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

Create:

**Epic:**

AI Case Review

Create 5 user stories:

For each:

1.  User role

2.  Goal

3.  Business value

4.  Acceptance criteria

Example:

\"As a medical reviewer, I want approval workflow so that high-risk
cases are validated before submission.\"

Today you learned:

✅ User story concept\
✅ Epic-feature-story hierarchy\
✅ INVEST principle\
✅ Agile requirement writing\
✅ Acceptance criteria connection\
✅ Healthcare AI user stories\
✅ Story splitting techniques

**Lesson 25 -- Acceptance Criteria Masterclass**

**Converting User Expectations into Clear, Testable Conditions**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--24

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 25 of 36**

Remaining:

**11 Lessons Left**

**1. What are Acceptance Criteria?**

**Definition**

Acceptance Criteria are a set of conditions that define when a user
story or requirement is considered complete and acceptable by the
business.

Simple:

Acceptance Criteria answer:

\"How will we know this feature is working correctly?\"

Example:

User Story:

As a safety reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can review
cases faster.

Without Acceptance Criteria:

Developer may create any type of summary.

With Acceptance Criteria:

The team knows exactly what is expected.

**2. Why are Acceptance Criteria Important?**

Acceptance criteria help:

✅ Remove ambiguity\
✅ Align BA, Product Owner, Developer, QA\
✅ Create test cases\
✅ Define completion\
✅ Reduce disputes

Example:

Requirement:

\"System should send notifications.\"

Questions:

-   When?

-   To whom?

-   What type?

-   What happens if delivery fails?

Acceptance criteria clarify these.

**3. Acceptance Criteria in Agile Flow**

Business Need

↓

User Story

↓

Acceptance Criteria

↓

Development

↓

Testing

↓

Acceptance

↓

Release

**4. Who Creates Acceptance Criteria?**

Usually:

-   Business Analyst

-   Product Owner

-   Business Stakeholders

-   QA Team

Collaboration:

BA:

Defines business expectation.

Developer:

Checks feasibility.

QA:

Creates test scenarios.

**5. Types of Acceptance Criteria**

There are three major types.

**Type 1: Scenario-Based Acceptance Criteria**

Most common.

Uses:

**Given -- When -- Then**

(Gherkin format)

Structure:

Given

Initial condition

When

User action

Then

Expected result

Example:

**User Story:**

As a safety reviewer, I want to upload safety reports.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given the user is logged in

When the user uploads a valid PDF report

Then the system should store the document successfully

And display confirmation message.

**Type 2: Rule-Based Acceptance Criteria**

Used for business rules.

Example:

Requirement:

High-risk cases require approval.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given risk score is above 80%

When reviewer submits case

Then medical approval should be required.

**Type 3: Checklist-Based Acceptance Criteria**

Used for simple features.

Example:

Login Feature:

Acceptance:

☑ User enters username\
☑ User enters password\
☑ Invalid login shows error\
☑ Successful login opens dashboard

**6. Functional Acceptance Criteria**

Defines system behavior.

Example:

User Story:

AI should generate risk score.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given a case has been analyzed

When reviewer opens case details

Then risk score should be displayed.

**7. Non-Functional Acceptance Criteria**

Defines quality expectations.

Examples:

-   Performance

-   Security

-   Availability

-   Usability

Example:

Performance:

Given dashboard has loaded

When user opens case list

Then data should display within 3 seconds.

Security:

Given user does not have permission

When accessing restricted case

Then system should deny access.

**8. Positive and Negative Acceptance Criteria**

A professional BA always covers both.

**Positive Scenario**

Expected behavior.

Example:

Valid document upload.

Given PDF file is valid

When user uploads

Then upload succeeds.

**Negative Scenario**

Error handling.

Example:

Invalid document.

Given file format is unsupported

When user uploads

Then system displays error message.

**9. Acceptance Criteria Example -- AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

**User Story:**

As a safety reviewer,

I want AI-generated case summaries,

so that I can review reports faster.

**Acceptance Criteria:**

**AC-001: Generate Summary**

Given a safety report is uploaded

When AI analysis completes

Then the system should generate a summary.

**AC-002: Display Important Information**

Given summary is generated

When reviewer opens case

Then system should display:

\- Drug information

\- Patient details

\- Adverse event information

**AC-003: AI Explanation**

Given AI provides risk classification

When reviewer views assessment

Then explanation should be available.

**AC-004: Processing Failure**

Given AI processing fails

When reviewer opens case

Then system should show failure status and retry option.

**10. Acceptance Criteria vs Definition of Done**

Important Agile interview question.

**Acceptance Criteria**

Specific to a user story.

Example:

\"Risk score should be displayed.\"

**Definition of Done**

Applies to all work.

Example:

-   Code reviewed

-   Testing completed

-   Documentation updated

-   Product owner approval received

Comparison:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Acceptance Criteria**       **Definition of Done**
  ----------------------------- -----------------------------------------
  Feature-specific              Overall completion standard

  Story level                   Sprint level

  Written by BA/PO              Team agreement
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**11. Writing Good Acceptance Criteria**

**Rule 1:**

Must be testable.

Bad:

\"System should be user-friendly.\"

Good:

\"User should complete case submission within 5 steps.\"

**Rule 2:**

Avoid technical implementation.

Bad:

\"Use React component.\"

Good:

\"Dashboard should display case status.\"

**Rule 3:**

Cover edge cases.

Example:

What if:

-   Data missing?

-   AI fails?

-   User unauthorized?

**Rule 4:**

Keep language simple.

**12. Common BA Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Writing acceptance criteria after development starts.

**Mistake 2:**

Making criteria too broad.

**Mistake 3:**

Ignoring negative scenarios.

**Mistake 4:**

Writing technical solutions.

**13. BA + QA Collaboration**

Acceptance criteria become test cases.

Example:

Acceptance Criteria:

\"System should block invalid files.\"

↓

QA Test Case:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Step**                              **Expected Result**
  ------------------------------------- ---------------------------------
  Upload invalid file                   Error shown

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**14. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What are acceptance criteria?**

Answer:

\"Acceptance criteria define the conditions that a product increment
must satisfy before a user story is accepted.\"

**Q2. Why use Given-When-Then?**

Answer:

\"Given-When-Then provides a structured way to describe scenarios
clearly and allows easy conversion into test cases.\"

**Q3. Difference between requirement and acceptance criteria?**

Answer:

\"Requirement explains what needs to be built, while acceptance criteria
define how we verify that it has been built correctly.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create acceptance criteria for:

**User Story:**

\"As a medical reviewer, I want to approve high-risk cases so that
regulatory decisions are properly validated.\"

Create:

1.  Positive scenario

2.  Negative scenario

3.  Business rule scenario

4.  Security scenario

✅ Given-When-Then format\
✅ Functional criteria\
✅ Non-functional criteria\
✅ Positive/negative scenarios\
✅ BA-QA collaboration\
✅ Healthcare AI examples

**Defining Quality, Performance, Security & Reliability Expectations**

✅ Lesson 1--25

Current:

**10 Lessons Left**

**1. What are Non-Functional Requirements (NFR)?**

**Definition**

Non-Functional Requirements define the quality attributes, performance
expectations, security standards, and operational characteristics of a
system.

Simple:

Functional Requirement:

What the system does.

Non-Functional Requirement:

How well the system should do it.

Example:

**Functional Requirement:**

\"The system shall generate AI risk scores.\"

**Non-Functional Requirement:**

\"The system shall generate risk scores within 30 seconds with 99%
availability.\"

**2. Functional vs Non-Functional Requirements**

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Functional Requirement**       **Non-Functional Requirement**
  -------------------------------- --------------------------------------
  System behavior                  System quality

  Features                         Performance & constraints

  User actions                     User experience

  What                             How well
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Example:

**Functional:**

User can upload adverse event reports.

**Non-Functional:**

System should support 10,000 simultaneous uploads.

**3. Why Are NFRs Important?**

Without NFRs:

A system may work but fail in real-world usage.

Example:

AI system generates correct risk scores.

But:

-   Takes 10 minutes

-   Crashes frequently

-   Has weak security

The product fails.

NFRs ensure:

✅ Reliability\
✅ Security\
✅ Scalability\
✅ Better user experience\
✅ Regulatory compliance

**4. Major Categories of Non-Functional Requirements**

Industry BAs commonly use these categories:

1.  Performance

2.  Security

3.  Availability

4.  Reliability

5.  Scalability

6.  Usability

7.  Maintainability

8.  Compatibility

9.  Compliance

10. Auditability

**5. Performance Requirements**

**Definition:**

Defines how fast and efficiently the system responds.

Examples:

**Response Time**

\"The dashboard shall load within 3 seconds.\"

**Processing Time**

\"AI document analysis shall complete within 60 seconds.\"

**Throughput**

\"The system shall process 5,000 reports per hour.\"

Healthcare AI Example:

Bad:

\"AI should be fast.\"

Good:

\"The AI engine shall generate case summaries within 30 seconds for
documents up to 20 MB.\"

**6. Security Requirements**

Very important in healthcare.

Security covers:

-   Authentication

-   Authorization

-   Data protection

-   Encryption

-   Access control

Examples:

**Authentication**

\"Users shall authenticate using multi-factor authentication.\"

**Authorization**

\"Only medical reviewers can approve high-risk cases.\"

**Encryption**

\"Patient data shall be encrypted during storage and transmission.\"

Healthcare Example:

A safety reviewer should not access another company\'s patient data.

Requirement:

\"System shall enforce organization-level data isolation.\"

**7. Availability Requirements**

**Definition:**

How often the system should be operational.

Example:

\"The platform shall maintain 99.5% availability.\"

Calculation:

99.5% availability means approximately:

\~3.65 hours downtime per month.

Healthcare Example:

Regulatory reporting systems require high availability.

**8. Reliability Requirements**

**Definition:**

Ability of the system to operate consistently without failure.

Examples:

\"The system shall recover failed AI processing automatically.\"

\"The system shall not lose uploaded case information during failures.\"

Reliability Metrics:

-   Error rate

-   Recovery time

-   Failure frequency

**9. Scalability Requirements**

**Definition:**

Ability to handle increasing workload.

Example:

Today:

10,000 reports/month

Future:

100,000 reports/month

System should support growth.

Requirement:

\"The system shall support increasing case volume without performance
degradation.\"

**10. Usability Requirements**

Focus:

User experience.

Examples:

\"The reviewer shall complete case approval within five steps.\"

\"The system shall provide clear error messages.\"

\"The dashboard shall support users with limited technical knowledge.\"

**11. Maintainability Requirements**

Focus:

Ease of modification and support.

Examples:

-   Code should be modular.

-   System should support future AI model updates.

-   Changes should not impact existing functionality.

**12. Compatibility Requirements**

Defines supported environments.

Examples:

\"The application shall support Chrome, Edge, and Firefox browsers.\"

\"The mobile application shall support Android and iOS.\"

**13. Compliance Requirements**

Critical for healthcare products.

Examples:

-   Data privacy

-   Regulatory reporting

-   Audit requirements

Healthcare AI Example:

\"The system shall maintain complete audit history of AI-generated
recommendations.\"

**14. Auditability Requirements**

Very important in regulated industries.

System should record:

-   Who performed action

-   What changed

-   When it changed

-   Previous value

-   New value

Example:

Before:

Risk Score = Medium

After:

Risk Score = High

System records:

User:\
Dr. Smith

Time:\
10:30 AM

Reason:\
Updated medical assessment

**15. AI-Specific Non-Functional Requirements**

For AI products, additional NFRs are needed.

**1. AI Accuracy**

Example:

\"AI classification accuracy should be monitored continuously.\"

**2. Explainability**

Example:

\"System shall provide reasons behind AI recommendations.\"

**3. Bias Monitoring**

Example:

\"AI model performance shall be evaluated across different datasets.\"

**4. Human Oversight**

Example:

\"Critical safety decisions require human approval.\"

**5. Model Performance Monitoring**

Example:

\"System shall track AI prediction confidence scores.\"

**16. Writing Professional NFRs**

Use:

\"The system shall\...\"

Bad:

\"The system should be secure.\"

Good:

\"The system shall encrypt sensitive data using approved encryption
standards.\"

Bad:

\"The system should be scalable.\"

Good:

\"The system shall support 100,000 monthly cases without degradation.\"

**17. Complete Healthcare AI NFR Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Performance**

NFR-001:

System shall generate AI summaries within 30 seconds.

**Security**

NFR-002:

System shall encrypt patient-related information.

**Availability**

NFR-003:

System shall maintain 99.5% uptime.

**Scalability**

NFR-004:

System shall support 10 times current case volume.

**Audit**

NFR-005:

System shall maintain complete user activity logs.

**Explainability**

NFR-006:

System shall display AI reasoning for every risk prediction.

**18. NFR Traceability**

Professional BA connects:

Business Need

↓

NFR

↓

User Story

↓

Acceptance Criteria

↓

Test Case

Example:

Business Need:

Protect patient data.

↓

NFR:

Encrypt patient information.

↓

Acceptance Criteria:

Given sensitive data is stored,

Then data must be encrypted.

**19. Common BA Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Ignoring NFRs.

**Mistake 2:**

Using vague words:

-   Fast

-   Secure

-   Easy

**Mistake 3:**

Not making NFR measurable.

**Mistake 4:**

Missing compliance requirements.

**20. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What are NFRs?**

Answer:

\"Non-functional requirements define system quality attributes such as
performance, security, scalability, reliability, and usability.\"

**Q2. Give examples of NFRs.**

Answer:

\"Response time, availability, security, scalability, auditability, and
compliance requirements are examples of NFRs.\"

**Q3. Why are NFRs important in healthcare systems?**

Answer:

\"Healthcare systems handle sensitive data and require high reliability,
security, compliance, and auditability.\"

**Practical Assignment**

Create NFRs for:

**AI Pharmacovigilance Platform**

Write minimum:

**Performance:**

2 requirements

**Security:**

2 requirements

**Availability:**

1 requirement

**Scalability:**

1 requirement

**AI Explainability:**

1 requirement

**Compliance:**

1 requirement

Today you learned:

✅ Functional vs Non-functional requirements\
✅ Performance requirements\
✅ Security requirements\
✅ Availability & reliability\
✅ Scalability\
✅ Usability\
✅ Compliance\
✅ AI-specific NFRs\
✅ Healthcare AI examples

**How a Business Analyst Works Inside an Agile Scrum Team**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

✅ Lesson 1--26

**1. Why Does a BA Need to Understand Scrum?**

Modern organizations rarely use only traditional waterfall projects.

Most companies use:

-   Scrum

-   SAFe

-   Hybrid Agile

-   Product-based delivery

A BA must understand:

-   How requirements flow

-   How teams collaborate

-   How value is delivered

**2. Scrum Team Structure**

A Scrum Team has three main accountabilities:

Scrum Team

\|

\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\-\--

\| \| \|

Product Owner Scrum Master Developers

Where does BA fit?

A BA can work in different ways:

**Model 1:**

BA supports Product Owner

(Common in enterprise)

**Model 2:**

BA works as part of delivery team

**Model 3:**

BA performs Product Analyst role

**3. BA vs Product Owner vs Scrum Master**

Very important interview topic.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Role**         **Main Focus**
  ---------------- ------------------------------------------------------
  Product Owner    What and Why to build

  Scrum Master     How team follows Scrum

  BA               Analyze needs and translate them into requirements

  Developers       Build solution
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**Example:**

Feature:

AI Risk Assessment Dashboard

**Product Owner:**

\"Users need AI risk visibility.\"

Defines:

-   Priority

-   Business value

**BA:**

Analyzes:

-   User needs

-   Workflow

-   Requirements

-   Acceptance criteria

**Scrum Master:**

Ensures:

-   Scrum events happen

-   Team collaboration

**Developers:**

Build dashboard.

**4. BA Activities Before Sprint**

A BA\'s work starts before development.

**Activity 1: Requirement Discovery**

BA understands:

-   Business problem

-   User needs

-   Stakeholder expectations

Example:

Problem:

Safety reviewers cannot prioritize cases.

**Activity 2: Requirement Analysis**

BA converts:

Business need →

Feature →

User Story

Example:

Epic:

AI Case Review

↓

Feature:

Risk Classification

↓

Story:

\"As a reviewer, I want risk scores\...\"

**Activity 3: Backlog Preparation**

BA helps create:

-   User stories

-   Acceptance criteria

-   Dependencies

-   Business rules

**Activity 4: Backlog Refinement Support**

BA ensures stories are:

✅ Clear\
✅ Complete\
✅ Understandable

**5. BA Role During Sprint**

During sprint execution, BA continues collaboration.

**1. Clarifying Requirements**

Developer asks:

\"What happens if AI confidence is low?\"

BA explains:

\"Cases below 70% confidence require manual review.\"

**2. Supporting Developers**

BA helps with:

-   Business rules

-   User workflows

-   Edge cases

**3. Supporting QA**

BA helps create:

-   Test scenarios

-   Expected behavior

Example:

Acceptance Criteria:

\"If risk score \>80%, approval required.\"

QA creates test:

Input:

Risk score 85%

Expected:

Approval workflow appears.

**4. Reviewing Completed Features**

BA checks:

Does feature solve user problem?

Example:

Developer built dashboard.

BA checks:

-   Are high-risk cases visible?

-   Is explanation shown?

-   Can reviewer complete task?

**6. BA Role in Scrum Ceremonies**

A BA participates in all important Scrum events.

**1. Sprint Planning**

BA helps:

-   Explain requirements

-   Clarify acceptance criteria

-   Discuss dependencies

Example:

Story:

AI Summary Feature

BA explains:

\"The summary must include drug, event, and severity information.\"

**2. Daily Scrum**

Usually developers lead.

BA may join to:

-   Answer questions

-   Remove requirement confusion

**3. Sprint Review**

BA helps demonstrate:

-   Completed functionality

-   Business value

Example:

\"Reviewer can now identify high-risk cases faster.\"

**4. Sprint Retrospective**

BA contributes improvement ideas.

Example:

\"Acceptance criteria should be created earlier.\"

**7. BA and Product Backlog**

A BA does not usually own the backlog, but supports backlog quality.

Product Owner owns:

-   Priority

-   Business decisions

BA supports:

-   Requirement clarity

-   Story refinement

-   Analysis

Good backlog item:

Epic

↓

Feature

↓

User Story

↓

Acceptance Criteria

↓

Tasks

**8. BA Role in User Story Lifecycle**

Lifecycle:

Idea

↓

Analyze

↓

Write Story

↓

Add Acceptance Criteria

↓

Refine

↓

Develop

↓

Test

↓

Accept

BA involvement:

Almost every stage.

**9. Healthcare AI Scrum Example**

**Product:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

**Sprint Goal:**

Reduce manual case review effort.

**Sprint Stories:**

Story 1:

Upload safety report.

Story 2:

Extract drug information.

Story 3:

Generate AI summary.

Story 4:

Display risk score.

BA Activities:

Before Sprint:

-   Interview reviewers

-   Define stories

-   Create acceptance criteria

During Sprint:

-   Clarify AI workflow

-   Support developers

After Sprint:

-   Validate feature with users

**10. BA Deliverables in Agile**

A BA may create:

**Before Sprint:**

-   User stories

-   Acceptance criteria

-   Process diagrams

-   Requirement notes

**During Sprint:**

-   Clarifications

-   Updated requirements

-   Decision logs

**After Sprint:**

-   UAT support

-   Release notes

-   Documentation

**11. Common BA Mistakes in Scrum**

**Mistake 1:**

Thinking BA disappears in Agile.

Wrong.

Agile needs analysis continuously.

**Mistake 2:**

Writing stories without user value.

**Mistake 3:**

Creating unclear acceptance criteria.

**Mistake 4:**

Not collaborating with developers.

**12. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is the role of BA in Scrum?**

Answer:

\"A BA helps understand business needs, analyze requirements, create
user stories, define acceptance criteria, and collaborate with
stakeholders and delivery teams.\"

**Q2. Does Scrum have a BA role?**

Answer:

\"Scrum does not officially define a BA role, but organizations often
use BAs to support Product Owners and development teams.\"

**Q3. Difference between BA and Product Owner?**

Answer:

\"Product Owner focuses on product vision and prioritization, while BA
focuses on detailed analysis and translating needs into actionable
requirements.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create Sprint Preparation Activities.

**Sprint Goal:**

\"Improve AI-assisted case review.\"

Prepare:

1.  Epic

2.  Feature

3.  Three User Stories

4.  Acceptance Criteria

5.  Dependencies

Today you learned:

✅ BA role in Scrum\
✅ BA vs PO vs Scrum Master\
✅ Before sprint activities\
✅ During sprint responsibilities\
✅ Scrum ceremonies\
✅ Backlog collaboration\
✅ Healthcare Agile example

**Lesson 28 -- Backlog Refinement Masterclass**

**How a BA Converts Ideas into Development-Ready User Stories**

**Module Progress Reminder**

Your roadmap:

**Total Lessons:** 36

Completed:

✅ Lesson 1--27

Current:

➡️ **Lesson 28 of 36**

Remaining:

**8 Lessons Left**

**1. What is Backlog Refinement?**

**Definition**

Backlog Refinement (also called Backlog Grooming) is the Agile activity
where the Scrum team reviews, analyzes, clarifies, prioritizes, and
prepares upcoming backlog items before they enter a sprint.

Simple:

Backlog refinement means:

**Making sure the team is ready before development starts.**

Without refinement:

Developer:

\"What exactly should I build?\"

QA:

\"How do I test this?\"

Product Owner:

\"I thought it meant something else.\"

With refinement:

Everyone understands:

-   What to build

-   Why to build

-   How to validate

**2. Why is Backlog Refinement Important?**

A good refinement process ensures:

✅ Clear requirements\
✅ Smaller stories\
✅ Better estimates\
✅ Fewer sprint blockers\
✅ Better collaboration\
✅ Faster delivery

**3. Who Participates in Backlog Refinement?**

Typical participants:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Role**                       **Contribution**
  ------------------------------ ----------------------------------------
  Product Owner                  Business priority

  BA                             Requirement clarity

  Developers                     Technical feasibility

  QA                             Testability

  UX Designer                    Design requirements

  Scrum Master                   Facilitation
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

**4. BA Role in Backlog Refinement**

The BA is one of the most important contributors.

**BA Responsibility 1:**

**Explain Business Context**

Not only:

\"What to build\"

but:

\"Why it matters.\"

Example:

Story:

\"Display AI risk score.\"

BA explains:

\"Reviewers currently spend hours identifying critical cases. Risk score
helps prioritize.\"

**BA Responsibility 2:**

**Clarify User Stories**

BA checks:

-   Is user clear?

-   Is goal clear?

-   Is value clear?

Bad Story:

As a user, I want reports.

Questions:

Which user?

What reports?

Why?

Improved:

As a safety manager, I want compliance reports so that I can monitor
regulatory submissions.

**BA Responsibility 3:**

**Define Acceptance Criteria**

BA ensures:

Developer knows expected behavior.

Example:

Story:

AI summary generation.

Acceptance Criteria:

Given a report is uploaded

When AI analysis completes

Then summary should display key safety information.

**BA Responsibility 4:**

**Identify Missing Requirements**

BA asks:

-   What happens if data is missing?

-   What happens if AI fails?

-   Who can access this feature?

-   What are business rules?

**5. Backlog Refinement Process**

Typical flow:

Review Epic

↓

Understand Business Goal

↓

Break into Features

↓

Create User Stories

↓

Split Large Stories

↓

Add Acceptance Criteria

↓

Identify Dependencies

↓

Estimate Effort

↓

Ready for Sprint

**6. Story Readiness Checklist**

Before sprint, a story should be:

**Clear**

Everyone understands it.

**Valuable**

Has business/user value.

**Small**

Can fit inside sprint.

**Testable**

Has acceptance criteria.

**Feasible**

Team can build it.

**7. Definition of Ready (DoR)**

Very important Agile concept.

Definition:

Definition of Ready is the checklist that determines whether a backlog
item is prepared for development.

A story is ready when:

✅ User story written\
✅ Acceptance criteria defined\
✅ Dependencies identified\
✅ UX requirements available\
✅ Business rules documented\
✅ Team understands requirement

Example:

Before:

\"Build AI dashboard.\"

Not ready.

After:

\"Create reviewer dashboard showing pending cases, risk score, and
priority.\"

Ready.

**8. Story Splitting Techniques**

Large stories create problems.

**Example:**

Large Story:

As a reviewer, I want complete AI case management.

Too large.

Split:

**Story 1:**

Upload safety report.

**Story 2:**

Extract report information.

**Story 3:**

Generate AI summary.

**Story 4:**

Display risk score.

**Story 5:**

Approve case.

**9. Backlog Refinement Example -- Healthcare AI**

**Epic:**

AI Pharmacovigilance Case Review

**Feature:**

AI Case Prioritization

**Initial Story:**

\"Improve case prioritization.\"

Too vague.

BA Refinement:

User:

Safety Reviewer

Goal:

View priority cases

Value:

Review serious cases faster

Final Story:

As a safety reviewer, I want AI-generated case priority levels so that I
can focus on high-risk adverse events first.

Acceptance Criteria:

**AC-001**

Given case analysis is completed

When reviewer opens dashboard

Then priority level should be displayed.

**AC-002**

High-risk cases should appear at top.

**AC-003**

Reviewer can filter cases by priority.

**10. Dependency Identification**

BA identifies dependencies.

Example:

Feature:

AI Risk Score

Dependencies:

-   AI model availability

-   Patient data access

-   Drug database integration

-   User permissions

Why important?

Avoid sprint delays.

**11. Estimation Readiness**

BA does not estimate technical effort.

But helps make stories understandable.

Example:

Bad:

\"Create AI system.\"

Impossible to estimate.

Good:

\"Create API to display AI risk score.\"

Can be estimated.

**12. Refinement Meeting Simulation**

**Story:**

AI Summary Generation

**BA:**

\"Reviewer needs automated summary because manual review takes 30
minutes.\"

**Developer:**

\"Which documents are supported?\"

**BA:**

\"PDF and XML initially.\"

**QA:**

\"What happens if extraction fails?\"

**BA:**

\"System should display error and allow retry.\"

**UX:**

\"Where should summary appear?\"

**BA:**

\"Case details page.\"

Result:

Ready story.

**13. Common Backlog Refinement Mistakes**

**Mistake 1:**

Refining too late.

**Mistake 2:**

Discussing solutions before understanding problem.

**Mistake 3:**

Stories are too big.

**Mistake 4:**

No acceptance criteria.

**Mistake 5:**

Ignoring dependencies.

**14. Interview Questions**

**Q1. What is backlog refinement?**

Answer:

\"Backlog refinement is the process of reviewing and preparing backlog
items by clarifying requirements, adding acceptance criteria, splitting
stories, and ensuring readiness for future sprints.\"

**Q2. What is Definition of Ready?**

Answer:

\"Definition of Ready is a checklist that confirms a user story has
enough information to enter a sprint.\"

**Q3. What is BA\'s role in backlog refinement?**

Answer:

\"BA helps clarify requirements, define acceptance criteria, identify
gaps, split stories, and ensure the team understands business needs.\"

**Practical Assignment**

For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

Create a refinement session.

Take this Epic:

**Epic:**

AI-Assisted Safety Review

Create:

1.  Two Features

2.  Five User Stories

3.  Acceptance Criteria

4.  Dependencies

5.  Definition of Ready checklist
